-length even if the minimizing operation considers integral moving cut without length constraint.

We say
G
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander
w.r.t.
A
if
\mathrm{cond}_{(h,s)}(A)\geq\phi
.

3.13
Flow Characterization of Length-Constrained Expanders
[
HRG22
]
.

If
G
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander w.r.t.
A
, then every
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
can be routed on
G
with congestion
O(\log n/\phi)
and length at most
sh
.

For a graph
G
and node-weighting
A
, if every
h
-length
A
-respecting demand can be routed on
G
with congestion
1/(2\phi)
and length
sh/2
, then
G
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander w.r.t.
A
.

Based on the flow characterization of length-constrained expanders, the following object called
expansion witness
can certify that a graph is an length-constrained expander, as shown in
Lemma

3.16
. Before that, we first define the notion of
embedding
. Intuitively, an embedding is just an integral and discretized interpretation of routing.

3.14
Embedding.

Let
G
be a graph with edge lengths. Let
G_{D}
be a unit-edge-length multi-graph with a mapping
\tau:V(G_{D})\to V(G)
. An embedding of
G_{D}
into
G
, denoted by
\Pi_{G_{D}\to G}
, is a mapping from
E(G_{D})
to simple paths (called
embedding paths
) on
G
, s.t. each edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G_{D})
is mapped to a simple path
\Pi_{G_{D}\to G}(e)
connecting vertices
\tau(u),\tau(v)\in V(G)
.

•

The embedding
\Pi_{G_{D}\to G}
has length
h
if all embedding paths have length at most
h
on
G
.

•

The embedding
\Pi_{G_{D}\to G}
has congestion
\gamma
if each edge
e\in E(G)
is contained by at most
\gamma
embedding paths.

We say
\Pi_{G_{D}\to G}
is an
(h,\gamma)
-embedding if it has length
h
and congestion
\gamma
.

Definition 3.15

(Expansion Witness)
.

Let
G
be a graph. Let
A
be a node weighting. An
(h,h_{{\cal R}},\gamma_{{\cal R}},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witness
of
A
in
G
is a tuple
({\cal N},\mathcal{R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
.

•

{\cal N}
is a neighborhood cover of
\mathrm{supp}(A)
on
G
with covering radius
h
.

•

\mathcal{R}=\{R^{S}\mid S\in{\cal N}\}
is a collection of routers. For each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
, its corresponding router
R^{S}\in{\cal R}
is a
(h_{{\cal R}},\gamma_{{\cal R}})
-router
R^{S}\in{\cal R}
with
V(R^{S})=S
for node-weighting
A_{S}=A_{\mid S}
(the restriction of
A
on
S
).

•

\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
is an
(h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-embedding of the union of all routers (i.e.
\bigcup_{R\in{\cal R}}R
) to
G
. Without ambiguity,
{\cal R}
also refers to
\bigcup_{R\in{\cal R}}R
. Note that the embedding uses the mapping
\tau:V({\cal R})\to V(G)
which is naturally defined by the fact that
V({\cal R})\subseteq V(G)
.

3.16
Expansion Witness Certifies Expansion.

Let
G
be a graph with node-weighting
A
. Suppose that there exists a
(h,h_{{\cal R}},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witness

({\cal N},\mathcal{R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
in
G
. Then, for an arbitrary
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
, it can be routed on
G
with length
h_{{\cal R}}\cdot h_{\Pi}
and congestion
\gamma_{\cal R}\cdot\gamma_{\Pi}
.

Proof.

We assign demand pairs of
D
to clusters in
{\cal N}
as follows. For each demand pair
(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(D)
, we assign it to an arbitrary cluster
S\in{\cal N}
s.t.
u,v\in S
. Note that
S
must exist because
D
is
h
-length on
G
and
{\cal N}
has covering radius
h
.

For each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
, let
D_{S}:\binom{S}{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the total demand of pairs assigned to
S
. Consider the router
R\in{\cal R}
corresponding to
S
. Observe that
D_{S}
is a
A_{S}
-respecting demand, so it can be routed on
R
with length
h_{{\cal R}}
and congestion
\gamma_{\cal R}
, by
Definition

3.9
. Finally, because all routers can be embedded into
G
simultaneously with length
h_{\Pi}
and congestion
\gamma_{\Pi}
,
D=\sum_{S\in{\cal N}}D_{S}
can be routed on
G
with length
h_{{\cal R}}\cdot h_{\Pi}
and congestion
\gamma_{\cal R}\cdot\gamma_{\Pi}
.
∎

3.17
Witnessed Expander Decomposition.

Let
G
be a graph with node-weighting
A
. A
(h,h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witnessed expander decomposition
(
(h,h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witnessed-ED for short) of
A
on
G
includes an integral moving cut
C
and an
(h,h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witness
({\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G-C})
of
A
on
G-C
.

Theorem 3.18

(Algorithmic Witnessed Expander Decomposition
[
HHT23
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph with edge lengths and node-weighting
A
. Given parameters
h
and
\phi
, there exists a deterministic algorithm that computes an
(h,h_{{\cal R}},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witnessed-ED
(C,{\cal N},\mathcal{R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G-C})
of
A
with the following additional guarantees.

•

|C|\leq\kappa_{\mathrm{ED},C}\cdot h\cdot\phi\cdot|A|
,

•

{\cal N}
has width
\omega=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
,

•

The total number of edges in all routers is
|E({\cal R})|\leq|E(G)|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
,

•

h_{{\cal R}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}
,
h_{\Pi}=\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}\cdot h
,
\gamma_{\cal R}=1
and
\gamma_{\Pi}=1/\phi
,

where
\kappa_{\mathrm{ED},C}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
are newly defined global parameters, and the global parameter
\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
is the same as that in
Theorem

3.6
. The running time is
|G|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

3.19
Expander Routing with Witness.

Let
G
be a graph with node-weighting
A
and an
(h,h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witness
({\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
. Let
\omega
be the width of
{\cal N}
. Given an
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
, there is an algorithm that computes an integral routing of
D
on
G
with length
\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot h_{\cal R}\cdot h_{\Pi}
and congestion
\kappa_{\mathrm{emb},\gamma}\cdot\gamma_{{\cal R}}\cdot\gamma_{\Pi}
, where
\lambda_{{\rm emb},h}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
and
\kappa_{{\rm emb},\gamma}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
are global parameters from
3.10
. The running time is
(\sum_{R\in{\cal R}}|R|+\omega\cdot|A|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h_{\cal R})\cdot h_{\Pi}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

Proof.

We use a similar strategy as the proof of
Lemma

3.16
. We assign each demand pair
(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(D)
to an arbitrary cluster
S\in{\cal N}
s.t.
u,v\in S
. Note that
S
must exist because
D
is
h
-length on
G
and
{\cal N}
has covering radius
h
. This step takes
\widetilde{O}(|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\leq\widetilde{O}(|A|)
time.

For each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
, let
D_{S}
be the total demand of pairs assigned to
S
. Let
R\in{\cal R}
be the router corresponding to
S
. Because
D_{S}
is a
A_{S}
-respecting demand, by applying

3.10
we can compute an integral routing
f_{S}
of
D_{S}
on
R
with congestion
\kappa_{\mathrm{emb},\gamma}\cdot\gamma_{\cal R}
and length
\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot h_{\cal R}
. Doing this for all clusters takes
\sum_{S\in{\cal N}}(|R^{S}|+|A_{S}|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h_{\cal R})\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}=(\sum_{R\in{\cal R}}|R|+\omega\cdot|A|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h_{\cal R})\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time.

Lastly, for each
D_{S}
and its routing
f_{S}
on
R^{S}
, we can extend
f_{S}
to be a routing on
G
. Precisely, for each path
P_{R}\in\mathrm{path}(f_{S})
on
R^{S}
, we substitute each edge
e\in P_{R}
with the embedding path
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}(e)
. The routing
f
of
D
on
G
is then
f=\bigcup_{S\in{\cal N}}f_{S}
. It obviously has congestion
\kappa_{\mathrm{emb},\gamma}\cdot\gamma_{\cal R}\cdot\gamma_{\Pi}
and length
\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot h_{\cal R}\cdot h_{\Pi}
on
G
. This step takes time
O(|\mathrm{path}(f)|\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot h_{\cal R}\cdot h_{\Pi})=|A|\cdot h_{\cal R}\cdot h_{\Pi}\cdot 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
time.
∎

3.7
Landmarks

In this subsection, we introduce a new concept of
landmarks
, which is for reducing the recourse in our dynamic algorithms.

3.20
Landmark Sets.

Given a graph
G
and an integral moving cut
C
, a vertex set
L\subseteq V(G)
is a
landmark set
of
C
with distortion
\sigma
if

•

for each
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
with
C(e)>0
and
\ell_{G-C}(e)>\sigma
, both endpoints
u,v\in L
;

•

for each
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
with
C(e)>0
and
\ell_{G-C}(e)\leq\sigma
, there exists
w_{u},w_{v}\in L
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,w_{u})\leq\sigma
and
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,w_{v})\leq\sigma
.

3.21
Union of Landmark sets.

Given a graph
G
and integral moving cuts
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
. Let
L_{1}
be a landmark set of
C_{1}
on
G
with distortion
\sigma_{1}
, and let
L_{2}
be a landmark set of
C_{2}
on
G-C_{1}
with distortion
\sigma_{2}
. Then
L=L_{1}\cup L_{2}
is a landmark set of
C=C_{1}+C_{2}
with distortion
\sigma=\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}
.

Proof.

Case 1.
For edges
e=(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{2})
. If
\ell_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(e)>\sigma
, then
\ell_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(e)>\sigma_{2}
and both
u,v\in L_{2}
. If
\ell_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(e)\leq\sigma
, there exists a landmark
w_{u}
of
u
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u,w_{u})\leq\sigma_{2}\leq\sigma
(the same holds for
v
).

Case 2.
Consider each edge
e=(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{1})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(C_{2})
. Note that
L_{1}
is a landmark set of
C_{1}
on
G
. If
\ell_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(e)>\sigma
, then
\ell_{G-C_{1}}(e)>\sigma\geq\sigma_{1}
(because
C_{2}(e)=0
) and both
u,v\in L_{1}
. From now we assume
\ell_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(e)\leq\sigma
, and in what follows, we will show that there is a landmark
w_{u}\in L
of
u
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(w_{u},u)\leq\sigma
. The same argument works for
v
.

First, there exists a landmark
w_{1,u}\in L_{1}
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}}(w_{1,u},u)\leq\sigma_{1}
. Consider the shortest
w_{1,u}
-
u
path
P_{1}
in
G-C_{1}
. If all edge
e\in P_{1}
has
C_{2}(e)=0
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(w_{1,u},u)\leq\sigma_{1}\leq\sigma
and we can take
w_{u}=w_{1,u}
as our desired landmark. Otherwise, let
u^{\prime}\in P_{1}
be the vertex closest to
u
and incident to an edge
e\in P_{1}
with
C_{2}(e)>0
. Observe that
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u,u^{\prime})=\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}}(u,u^{\prime})\leq\ell_{G-C_{1}}(P_{1})=\sigma_{1}
, because all edges
e
on the subpath of
P^{\prime}
from
u
to
u^{\prime}
have
C_{2}(e)=0
. Also, by the property of
L_{2}
, there exists
w_{2,u}\in L_{2}
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u^{\prime},w_{2,u})\leq\sigma_{2}
. Then
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u,w_{2,u})\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u,u^{\prime})+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{1}-C_{2}}(u^{\prime},w_{2,u})\leq\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}=\sigma
, so we can let
w_{u}
be
w_{2,u}
as the desired landmark.

∎

3.22
Update Landmark Sets Under Edge Deletions.

Given a graph
G
and an integral moving cut
C
. Let
L
be a landmark set of
C
on
G
with distortion
\sigma
. Given a batch of edge deletions
F\subseteq E(G)
,
\widetilde{L}=L\cup V(F)
is a landmark set of
\widetilde{C}=C\setminus F
on
\widetilde{G}=G\setminus F
with distortion
\sigma
.

Proof.

For each edge
e=(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{C})=\mathrm{supp}(C)\setminus F
, if
\ell_{\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{C}}(e)>\sigma
, then
\ell_{G-C}(e)=\ell_{\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{C}}(e)>\sigma
and
u,v
are already in
L
.

For each
C
-vertex
v
, there exists
w\in L
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,w)\leq\sigma
, and let
P
be the
v
-
w
shortest path in
G-C
. If
P
is disjoint with
F
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{C}}(v,w)=\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,w)
and
w
is still a valid landmark of
v
. Otherwise,
F
is on
P
and let
w^{\prime}
be the vertex in
P\cap V(F)
closest to
v
. Then
\mathrm{dist}_{\widetilde{G}-\widetilde{C}}(v,w^{\prime})\leq\ell_{G-C}(v,w)=\sigma
and we can take
w^{\prime}
as the new landmark of
v
.

∎

4
Local Length-Constrained Flows

In this section, we develop a local algorithm in
Theorem

4.3
for the approximate length-constrained maxflow problem by generalizing the results in
[
HHS23
]
. With this local flow algorithm, our second result is a local cutmatch algorithm shown in
Theorem

4.4
, which is an important subroutine for our dynamic expander decomposition algorithms in
Section

6
.

In the length-constrained maxflow problem, we work with a
directed
graph
G
with edge lengths
\ell
and edge capacities
U
, where the lengths and capacities are
positive
integers. During the algorithm, we may also assign
weights

w
to edges in
G
, and the weights are non-negative real numbers. For each vertex
v\in V(G)
, we let
E^{+}(v)
(resp.
E^{-}(v)
) denote the set of outgoing (resp. ingoing) edges of
v
. Let
\deg^{+}_{G}(v)=|E^{+}(v)|
be the out-degree of
v
in
G
. For a subset of vertices
B\subseteq V(G)
, we let
E^{+}(B)=\bigcup_{v\in B}E^{+}(v)
and
E^{-}(B)=\bigcup_{v\in B}E^{-}(v)
. Also, we use
N^{+}(B)=\{v\mid\exists(u,v)\in E(G)\text{ s.t. }u\in B\text{ and }v\in V(G)\setminus B\}
to denote the outgoing neighbors of
B
, and let
N^{+}[B]=N^{+}(B)\cup B
. Similarly,
N^{-}(B)=\{u\mid\exists(u,v)\in E(G)\text{ s.t. }u\in V(G)\setminus B\text{ and }v\in B\}
are the ingoing neighbors of
B
.

Furthermore, we will specify two vertices
s
and
t
be the source vertex and sink vertex. For each vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}
, we let
\Delta(v)=U(s,v)
be the
source capacity
of
v
, where
U(s,v)
is the capacity of the edge from
s
to
v
(if no such edge,
U(s,v)=0
). Similarly,
\nabla(v)=U(v,t)
denote the
sink capacity
of
v
. Edges in
E^{+}(s)
are called
source edges
and edges in
E^{-}(t)
are
sink edges
.

4.1
Single-Commodity Length-Constrained Flows.

Given a graph
G
with a source vertex
s
, a sink vertex
t
and a length parameter
h
, let
{\cal P}_{h}(s,t)
denote the set of simple
s
-to-
t
paths
P
with
\ell_{G}(P)\leq h
. An
(feasible)
h
-length
s
-
t
flow

f
on
G
assigns a non-negative real number
f(P)
to each
P\in{\cal P}_{h}(s,t)
s.t.
f(e)\leq U(e)
for each
e\in E(G)
, where
f(e)=\sum_{P\ni e}f(P)
denote the total flow value going through
e
. The
value
of
f
is
\mathrm{val}(f)=\sum_{P\in{\cal P}_{h}(s,t)}
. The flow
f
is
integral
if
f(P)
is an integer for each
P
. We use
\mathrm{path}(f)=\{P\in{\cal P}_{h}(s,t)\mid f(P)>0\}
to denote the
flow paths
of
f
and let
|\mathrm{path}(f)|
be the
path count
of
f
.

4.2
Fractional Moving Cut.

Given a graph
G
with a source vertex
s
, a sink vertex
t
and a length parameter
h
, a
(feasible)
h
-length fractional moving cut
is a function
w:E(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
s.t. for each path
P\in{\cal P}_{h}(s,t)
,
w(P)\geq 1
. The size of
w
is denoted by
|w|=\sum_{e\in E(G)}U(e)\cdot w(e)
.

Given graph
G
with source
s
, sink
t
and length parameter
h
, the (exact) length-constrained maxflow problem asks a feasible
h
-length
s
-
t
fractional flow
f
with maximum value, while the (exact) length-constrained mincut problems asks a feasible
h
-length fractional moving cut
w
with minimum size. These two problems are dual to each other as shown in
[
HHS23
]
. That is, the value of maximum
h
-length flow is the same as the size of minimum
h
-length moving cut.

Naturally, the approximate length-constrained maxflow problem asks a pair
(f,w)
of feasible
h
-length
s
-
t
maxflow
f
and minimum moving cut
w
. A solution
(f,w)
is
\alpha
-approximation if
|w|\leq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{val}(f)
. Namely, the moving cut
w
certifies the value of the length-constrained flow up to an
\alpha
factor.

Our result on local approximate length-constrained maxflow problem is formally stated in
Theorem

4.3
. The running time is local in the sense that if every vertex
v
has sink capacity
\nabla(v)=U(v,t)\geq\Omega(\deg^{+}_{G}(v))
at least a constant fraction of its out-degree, then the running time is proportional to the total source capacities, with overhead
\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)
. This notion of “local running time” appeared before in e.g.
[
SW19
]
. We point out that the condition
\nabla(v)\geq\Omega(\deg^{+}_{G}(v))
for all
v\in V(G)
is kind of crucial to obtain local running time depending on total source capacities instead of the original graph size. Intuitively, imagine a graph in which half of vertices have zero sink capacity. Then in the worst case, even finding a path from
s
to
t
may need to scan through all these zero-sink-capacitated vertices. The running time bound in
Theorem

4.3
is analysed in a general setting that some vertices
v
may have
\nabla(v)
much smaller than
\deg^{+}_{G}(v)
, which brings an extra term
\max\{\deg^{+}_{G}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v)\}
about the total
deficit
, which, roughly speaking, measures the total differences between
\deg^{+}_{G}(v)
and
\nabla(v)
over all vertices. Furthermore, the requirement that all sink edges have equal length is also unavoidable, because otherwise we can easily reduce the following toy problem to an
h
-length maxflow problem: given
n
(different) integers, answer whether there is an integer at most
h
or not, which is impossible to be solved in local time.

In fact,
Theorem

4.3
includes two solutions with different approximation ratio, one is
(\log n/\delta)
-approximate and the other one is
O(2+\delta)
-approximate. Our local cutmatch algorithm (i.e.
Theorem

4.4
) will use the
O(\log n)
-approximate and exploit the additional guarantee. We include the
(2+\delta)
-approximate result since it may be of independent interest.

4.3
Local Approximate Length-Constrained Maxflow.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a directed graph with lengths
\ell
, capacities
U
, source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
and parameters
\delta>0,h\geq 1
, further satisfying that each sink edge
e\in E^{-}(t)
has
\ell(e)=1
. With access to the adjacency list of
G
, there is an algorithm that compute

1.

a feasible
h
-length flow and moving cut pair
(f,w)
that is
O(\log n/\delta)
-approximate, further satisfying that, for some
\zeta=1/(6\delta)+3
and
\eta=\Theta(\delta^{2}/\log n)
,
f/\eta
is an integral flow and
w(e)=(1+\delta/6)^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}/m^{\zeta}
for each
e\in E(G)
,

2.

a feasible
h
-length flow and moving cut pair
(f,w_{\min})
that is
(2+\delta)
-approximate.

The running time is

\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(c\cdot\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v),0\}),

where
c\geq 1
is a parameter arbitrarily chosen.

In
Section

4.3
, we will use the local length-constrained flow algorithm to compute
cutmatches
, which was first introduced in
[
HRG22
]
with applications on the first efficient length-constrained expander decomposition algorithm. The notion of cutmatch is defined on an
undirected

uncapacitated
graph
G
with edge lengths
\ell_{G}
. The formal definition is given in
Theorem

4.4
. Roughly speaking, cutmatch is made up with a matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
and a moving cut
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
. The matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
will partially matches two given node-weightings via low-congestion
h
-length paths on
G
, and the moving cut certifies that the matching can be hardly extended under the low-congestion requirement. We point out that an efficient (global) cutmatch algorithm has already been shown in
[
HHS23
]
. Here we design a cutmatch algorithm with local running time. Moreover, it is adapted to be compatible with the algorithms in
Section

6
. For example,
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
will be rounded to an integral moving cut, and we also output a landmark set
L_{\mathrm{CM}}
(see
Definition

3.20
) of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

4.4
Local Cutmatches.

Let
G
be a graph with source node-weighting
A^{\mathrm{src}}
, and sink node-weighting
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
s.t.
A^{\mathrm{src}}+A^{\mathrm{sink}}\geq\deg_{G}+\mathds{1}(V(G))
. Given parameters
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}
, there is an algorithm that computes the following:

•

a partition of
A^{\mathrm{src}}
into
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}\subseteq A^{\mathrm{src}}
,

•

a partition of
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
into
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}\subseteq A^{\mathrm{sink}}
s.t.
|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|\leq|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|
and
|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|=O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|)
,

•

a set of
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length paths which embeds a matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
between
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
with size
|M_{\mathrm{CM}}|=|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|
and congestion
\gamma_{\mathrm{CM}}=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}})
where
\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}=\log^{4}n
,

•

an integral moving cut
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
with size
|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq O(\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|)
s.t.
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}
are
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separated on
G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

•

a landmark set
L_{\mathrm{CM}}
of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
on
G
with distortion
\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\kappa_{\sigma}
and size
|L_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|
, where
\kappa_{\sigma}=n^{\epsilon^{4}}
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}=O(\kappa_{\sigma}\cdot\log^{3}n)
.

The running time is
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}})\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

Here we make some further explanations on the terminology in
Theorem

4.4
. Recall that
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
also refer to sets of virtual nodes (see
Definition

3.1
), so partitions on
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
are well-defined. The matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
between
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
is actually an unweighted matching between virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
.

4.5
.

We want to emphasize that, although in
Theorem

4.4
and its proof we talk about node-weighting and virtual nodes,
Theorem

4.4
also holds when the input
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
are general integral function of
V(G)
. In particular, in
Theorem

7.5
, we will apply
Theorem

4.4
with
density functions
and
items
(analogous to node-weightings and virtual nodes) as input.

4.1
Local
h
-Length Lightest Path Blockers

In
[
HHS23
]
, it is shown that the approximate length-constrained maxflows can be reduced to computing lightest path blockers via a multiplicative weight update framework. In this subsection, we localize the path blockers subroutine by invoking the (global) path blockers subroutine in
[
HHS23
]
as a blackbox.

4.6
Length-Constrained Lightest Path Blockers.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a graph with lengths
\ell
, weights
\omega
and capacities
U
. Given source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
, and parameters
\gamma_{2}\geq\gamma_{1}\geq 1
,
h\geq 1
,
\lambda\leq d_{w}^{(h)}(s,t)
, where
d_{w}^{(h)}(s,t)
denote the minimum weights among
h
-length
s
-
t
paths, an
h
-length integral
s
-
t
flow
f
is an
h
-length
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
-lightest path blocker
if

(1)

every path
P\in\mathrm{supp}(f)
has weight at most
\gamma_{2}\cdot\lambda
,

(2)

for each
h
-length
s
-
t
path
P^{\prime}
with weight at most
\gamma_{1}\cdot\lambda
, there exists
e\in P^{\prime}
s.t.
f(e)=U(e)
(we say that
f

blocks

P^{\prime}
in this case).

4.7
Theorem 11.1,
[
HHS23
]
.

Given a directed graph
G=(V(G),E(G))
with lengths
\ell
, weights
w
, capacities
U
, source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
and parameters
\delta>0
,
h\geq 1
and
\lambda\leq d_{w}^{(h)}(s,t)
, there is an algorithm that compute an
h
-length
(1+\delta,1+2\delta)
-lightest path blocker
f
.
The running time is
\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot|G|
.

4.8
.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a directed graph with lengths
\ell
, weights
w
, capacities
U
, source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
and parameters
\delta>0
,
h\geq 1
and
\lambda\leq d_{w}^{(h)}(s,t)
, further satisfying that,
(s,t)\notin E(G)
and each sink edge
e\in E^{-}(t)
has
w(e)=0
and
\ell(e)=1
. With access to the adjacency list of
G
, there is an algorithm that compute an
h
-length
(1+\delta,1+2\delta)
-lightest path blocker
f
. The running time is

\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(c\cdot\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v),0\}),

where
c\geq 1
is a parameter arbitrarily chosen.

Proof.

The Algorithm.
The algorithm is iterative. In the
i
-th iteration, we will construct some local graph
G_{i}
and compute a blocker
f_{i}
on
G_{i}
using
Theorem

4.7
. The final output is the accumulated flow
f=\sum_{i}f_{i}
.

Suppose we are now in the
i
-th iteration. The graph
G_{i}
is constructed as follows. Let
\hat{f}_{i-1}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\leq i-1}f_{i^{\prime}}
be the accumulated flow of the previous
i-1
iterations. In particular,
\hat{f}_{0}=0
is the zero flow. Let
U_{i}=U-\hat{f}_{i-1}
be the remaining capacity function. Let
V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}\subseteq\{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}\mid\hat{f}_{i-1}(v,t)=\nabla(v)\}
be vertices reachable from
s
via a BFS which only goes through non-saturated edges (i.e.
e
s.t.
U_{i}(e)>0
) and sink-saturated vertices (i.e.
v
s.t.
\hat{f}_{i-1}(v,t)=\nabla(v)
).
Then the vertex set and edge set of
G_{i}
are

V(G_{i})=\{s,t\}\cup N^{+}[\{s\}\cup V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}],

and

E(G_{i})=(\{(s,v),(v,t)\mid v\in V(G_{i})\setminus\{s,t\}\}\cap E(G))\cup E^{+}(V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}).

For each edge
e\in E(G_{i})
, we define its capacity be
U_{i}(e)=U(e)-\hat{f}_{i-1}(e)
and keep its weight and length unchanged, i.e.
w_{i}(e)=w(e)
and
\ell_{i}(e)=\ell(e)
.

After constructing
G_{i}
, we compute a blocker
f_{i}
on
G_{i}
using
Theorem

4.7
with parameters
h_{i}=i+1
and proceed to the next iteration. The whole algorithm will terminate right after the
h
-th iteration, and the output is
f=\hat{f}_{h}=\sum_{i\leq h}f_{i}
.

Proof of Correctness.
First, the flow paths in the final flow
f
indeed have weight at most
(1+2\delta)\cdot\lambda
because
Theorem

4.7
guarantees that flow paths in each
f_{i}
has weight at most
(1+2\delta)\cdot\lambda
. Next we show that
f
blocks all
h
-length path with weight at most
(1+\delta)\lambda
by induction. Let the induction hypothesis be that, right after each iteration
i
, all
(i+1)
-length
s
-
t
paths in
G
with weight at most
(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda
have been blocked by
\hat{f}_{i}
.

The Base Case.
Consider the first iteration. Note that every
2
-length path
P
in
G
must in the form
s\to v\to t
, because
(s,t)\notin E(G)
and every edge in
G
has positive integral length. Because
v\in N^{+}(\{s\})
,
P
is fully inside the local graph
G_{1}
. By
Theorem

4.7
, the path blocker
\hat{f}_{1}=f_{1}
will blocked all
2
-length paths in
G_{1}
with weight at most
(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda
, so every
2
-length path in
G
with weight at most
(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda
will be blocked by
\hat{f}_{1}
.

An Inductive Step.
Now consider iteration
i
with
i\geq 2
. Let
P=s\to v_{1}\to\cdots\to v_{k}\to t
be an
(i+1)
-length
s
-
t
path in
G
with weight at most
(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda
. If
P
has been blocked by
\hat{f}_{i-1}
in
G
, the trivially it will blocked by
\hat{f}_{i}
in
G
. Now suppose
P
has not been blocked by
\hat{f}_{i-1}
.

4.9
.

If
P
has not been blocked by
\hat{f}_{i-1}
, then
P\subseteq G_{i}
.

Proof.

We assume that
k\geq 2
without loss of generality. Because if
k=1
, then
P
is in the form
s\to v\to t
and it must be a path in
G_{i}
because
v\in N^{+}(\{s\})
.

We first show that each
v_{k^{\prime}}
with
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq k-1
is in
V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}
. Assume this is not true for contradiction. Let
k^{\prime}
be the smallest s.t.
v_{k^{\prime}}\notin V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}
, and we consider the path
P^{\prime}=s\to v_{1}\to\cdots\to v_{k^{\prime}}\to t
. We know that
P^{\prime}
has not been blocked by
\hat{f}_{i-1}
because the edges
(s,v_{1}),(v_{1},v_{2}),...,(v_{k^{\prime}-1},v_{k^{\prime}})
are not saturated (since
P
is not blocked by
\hat{f}_{i-1}
) and
(v_{k^{\prime}},t)
is not saturated (since
v_{k^{\prime}}\notin V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}
). However, we know that
P^{\prime}
is a
i
-length path because

\ell_{G}(P^{\prime})=\ell_{G}(P)-\ell_{G}(v_{k^{\prime}}\to\cdots\to v_{k})-\ell_{G}(v_{k},t)+\ell_{G}(v_{k^{\prime}},t),

where
\ell_{G}(v_{k},t)=\ell_{G}(v_{k^{\prime}},t)=1
from the theorem statement and
\ell_{G}(v_{k^{\prime}}\to\cdots\to v_{k})\geq 1
. The weight of
P^{\prime}
can be bounded by

w(P^{\prime})=w(P)-w(v_{k^{\prime}}\to\cdots\to v_{k})-w(v_{k},t)+w(v_{k^{\prime}},t)\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda.

because
w(P)\leq(1+\delta)\cdot\lambda
and
w(v_{k^{\prime}},t)=0
from the theorem statement. Therefore, the existence of
P^{\prime}
violates the induction hypothesis.

Providing that
v_{k^{\prime}}\in V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}
for all
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq k-1
, we know
P\subseteq G_{i}
because edges
(v_{1},v_{2}),...,(v_{k-1},v_{k})\in E^{+}(V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}})\subseteq E(G_{i})
, and
(s,v_{1}),(v_{k},t)\in E(G_{i})
(since
v_{1},v_{k}\in V(G_{i})
by definition).

∎

By
4.9
and
Theorem

4.7
,
f_{i}
will blocked
P
in
G_{i}
. Note that the capacity of
G_{i}
is defined by
U_{i}=U-\hat{f}_{i-1}
, so
\hat{f}_{i}=\hat{f}_{i-1}+f_{i}
will block
P
in
G
.

Time Analysis.
We now analyse the running time. We first bound the size of each
G_{i}
. Observe that
|V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}|\leq\deg^{+}_{G}(\{s\}\cup V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}})
by considering the BFS procedure constructing
V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}
. Thus, the number of vertices in
G_{i}
is

\displaystyle|V(G_{i})|\leq 2+|V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}|+\deg^{+}_{G}(\{s\}\cup V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}})\leq 2+2\cdot\deg^{+}_{G}(\{s\}\cup V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}).

Also, the number of edges in
G_{i}
is bounded by

\displaystyle|E(G_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq 2|V(G_{i})|+\deg^{+}_{G}(V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}})

\displaystyle\leq 4+3(\deg_{G}^{+}(s)+\deg^{+}_{G}(V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}))

\displaystyle\leq O(\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v))+O(\sum_{v\in V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}}c\cdot\nabla(v))+O(\sum_{v\in V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}}\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v),0\})

\displaystyle\leq O(\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(c\cdot\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v),0\}))

where the third inequality is by
\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v)\geq\deg^{+}_{G}(s)
because each source edge has capacity at least
1
(the capacity is a positive integer), and the last inequality we use that
\sum_{v\in V_{i-1,\mathrm{sat}}}\nabla(v)\leq\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v)
because the total capacity of saturated sinks is at most the total source.

The running time
i
-th iteration is dominated by applying
Theorem

4.7
on
G_{i}
, which is

\displaystyle~~~~\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot|G_{i}|

\displaystyle=\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(c\cdot\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg^{+}_{G}(v)-c\cdot\nabla(v),0\}).

Summing over
h
iterations gives the desired bound of total running time.

∎

4.10
.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a directed graph with lengths
\ell
, weights
w
, capacities
U
, source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
and parameters
\delta>0
,
h\geq 1
and
\lambda\leq d_{w}^{(h)}(s,t)
, further satisfying that each sink edge
e\in E^{-}(t)
has
\ell(e)=1
.
For each vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}
, let

\widetilde{\nabla}(v)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,\ \text{if $e=(v,t)$ does not exist or $w(v,t)>(1+\delta)\lambda$}\\
&\nabla(v),\ \text{if $w(v,t)\leq(1+\delta)\lambda$}\end{aligned}\right.

With access to the adjacency list of
G
, There is an algorithm that compute an
h
-length
(1+\delta,2+3\delta)
-lightest path blocker
f
with
|\mathrm{path}(f)|\leq\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v)
.
The running time is

\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(c\cdot\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-c\cdot\widetilde{\nabla}(v),0\}),

where
c\geq 1
is a parameter arbitrarily chosen.

Proof.

We consider a graph
\widetilde{G}
obtained by (1) removing all sink edges of
G
with weight larger than
(1+\delta)\lambda
and then (2) setting the weight of each remaining sink edge (with original weight at most
(1+\delta)\lambda
in
G
) to be zero. Note that for each
v\in V(\widetilde{G})\setminus\{s,t\}
, its source capacity is
\widetilde{\Delta}(v)=\Delta(v)
and its sink capacity is exactly
\widetilde{\nabla}(v)
defined in the statement of this corollary.

Now we apply
Theorem

4.8
on
G^{\prime}
with the same parameters
\delta,h
and
\lambda
. We will show that the output
f
is an
h
-length
(1+\delta,2+3\delta)
-lightest path blocker of
G
. First, every path
\widetilde{P}\in\mathrm{supp}(f)
has weight at most
(1+2\delta)\lambda
on
\widetilde{G}
. Every edge on
\widetilde{P}
has the same weight on
G
and
\widetilde{G}
, except that the only sink edge of
\widetilde{P}
has weight zero on
\widetilde{G}
but at most
(1+\delta)\lambda
on
G
. Hence
\widetilde{P}
has weight at most
(1+2\delta)\lambda+(1+\delta)\lambda=(2+3\delta)\lambda
on
G
. For the second property, consider an
h
-length
s
-
t
path
P
with weight at most
(1+\delta)\lambda
on
G
. The path
P
is totally inside
\widetilde{G}
because we only remove those sink edges with weight larger than
(1+\delta)\lambda
and they will not belong to
P
. Also, the weight of
P
on
\widetilde{G}
is still at most
(1+\delta)\lambda
, so
P
is blocked by
f
as desired.

The running time bound is straightforward by
Theorem

4.8
, and the path count
|\mathrm{path}(f)|
of
f
is at most the total source capacities
\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v)
because
f
is an integral flow.
∎

4.2
Local Approximate Length-Constrained Maxflows: Proof of
Theorem

4.3

In this subsection, we will adapt the multiplicative weight update framework in
[
HHS23
]
and use our local path blockers algorithm to achieve local running time for the length-constrained max flow problem.

Algorithm 1

Local Algorithms for Approximate Length-Constrained Maxflows and Moving Cuts

1:

A directed graph
G=(V(G),E(G))
with length
\ell
, capacities
U
, source vertex
s
, sink vertex
t
and an parameters
\delta>0
,
h\geq 1
.

2:

An
O(\log n/\delta)
-approximate pair
(f,w)
and a
(2+O(\delta))
-approximate pair
(f,w_{\min})
.

3:

Let
\delta_{0}=\frac{\delta}{6}
, let
\zeta=\frac{1+2\delta_{0}}{\delta_{0}}+1
and let
\eta=\frac{\delta_{0}}{(1+\delta_{0})\cdot\zeta}\cdot\frac{1}{\log m}
.

4:

Initialize
w(e)=1/m^{\zeta}
for all edges
e
in
G
.

5:

Initialize
\lambda=1/m^{\zeta}
.

6:

Initialize
w_{\min}=w/\lambda
.

7:

Initialize
f
to be zero flow.

8:

while

\lambda<1

do

9:

for

\Theta(h\log_{1+\delta_{0}}(n)/\delta_{0})
iterations
do

10:

Compute an
h
-length
(1+\delta_{0},2+3\delta_{0})
-lightest path blocker
\hat{f}
in
G
by applying
Corollary

4.10
, with length
\ell
, weight
w
, capacity
U
, and parameters
h,\lambda
as input.

11:

Update
f\leftarrow f+\eta\cdot\hat{f}
.

12:

For every
e\in E(G)
where
\hat{f}(e)>0
,
w(e)\leftarrow(1+\delta_{0})^{\hat{f}(e)/U(e)}\cdot w(e)
.

13:

If
|w|/\lambda<|w_{\min}|
,
w_{\min}\leftarrow w/\lambda
.

14:

end

for

15:

\lambda\leftarrow(1+\delta_{0})\cdot\lambda

16:

end

while

17:

Return
(f,w)
and
(f,w_{\min})
.

The Algorithm.

The detailed algorithm is shown in
Algorithm

1
. Roughly speaking, the algorithm just iteratively compute a path blocker
\hat{f}
on
G
by applying
Corollary

4.10
. The input functions are the original edge length function
\ell
, the edge weight function
w
(i.e. the current moving cut) and the original capacity function
U
as input functions. The input parameters are
h,\lambda
. After computing
\hat{f}
, we then update the flow solution
f
additively and update the moving cut solution
w
multiplicatively.

In what follows, we will discuss some implementation details to achieve local running time.

•

The flow solution
f
is stored in two representations. The first one is the
flow path representation
, which just stores each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(f)
and its flow value
f(P)
explicitly. The second one is the
edge function representation
, which stores all edges
e
s.t.
f(e)>0
and its flow value
f(e)
in binary search tree. Note that the space to store
f
in this way is obviously
O(h\cdot|\mathrm{path}(f)|)
, because each flow path has at most
h
edges (recall that each flow path is
h
-length and each edge has length at least 1). By the same reason, each additive update to
f
(line 8) just takes time
\widetilde{O}(h\cdot\mathrm{path}(\hat{f}))
.

•

The moving cut solution
w
will be stored in a similar
edge function representation
. we store all edges
e
and its weight
w(e)
s.t.
w(e)
is large than the default value
1/m^{\zeta}
into a binary search tree. Actually, edges
e
with
w(e)>1/m^{\zeta}
are exactly those edges
e
with
f(e)>0
by the simple invariant
w(e)=(1+\delta_{0})^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}
, so the space to store
w
is still
O(h\cdot|\mathrm{path}(f)|)
. The multiplicative update of
w
(line 9) can be done in
\widetilde{O}(h\cdot|\mathrm{path}(\hat{f})|)
time
12
12

12

Actually, a cleverer implementation is to use to the same binary search tree as the edge function representations of both
f
and
w
.
with access to the edge function representation of
\hat{f}
.

Proof of Correctness.

Intuitively,
w
is the cut solution at the end, while
w_{\min}
is the best cut solution over the course of the algorithm. By adapting the proof in
[
HHS23
]
, we can show that
(f,w_{\min})
is indeed a
(2+O(\delta))
-approximate solution. We omit the proof here because our local cutmatch algorithm does not use this solution. The approximation is
2+O(\delta)
instead of
1+\delta
in
[
HHS23
]
because our local path blockers (i.e.
Corollary

4.10
) have quality worse than the path blockers in
[
HHS23
]
.

In the remaining proof, we will formally prove that
(f,w)
is an
O(\log n/\delta)
-approximate solution. Our proof basically follows the presentation in
[
HHS23
]
. In particular,
4.11
and
4.12
are almost identical to Lemmas 12.1 and 12.2 in
[
HHS23
]
, but
4.13
is new.

We refer to the outer loop of
Algorithm

1
as one
phase
, and refer to the inner loop as one
iteration
. We will say
\lambda
is unchanged in one iteration, and
\lambda
is updated right before the end of one phase. Strictly speaking, for each phase, the end of this phase and the beginning of the next phase represent the same moment. However, for one iteration, the end of this iteration and the beginning of the next iteration may represent different moments, and
\lambda
may be updated between them.

4.11
.

At the beginning and the end of
Algorithm

1
, and at the end of each iteration, we have
\lambda\leq d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
.

Proof.

The statement trivially holds at the very beginning of the algorithm.

First, we prove
\lambda\leq d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)
. Note that
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)
will only increase, so it suffices to show that at the beginning of each phase,
\lambda\leq d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)
holds. We will show this by induction. Consider a phase. At the beginning and the end of this phase, we let
\lambda_{1}=\lambda
and
\lambda_{2}=(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
denote the value of
\lambda
, and similarly, we let
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
denote the value of
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)
. To make the induction work, it suffices to show
\lambda_{2}\leq d_{2}
given
\lambda_{1}\leq d_{1}
. Assume the opposite for contradiction. From
\lambda_{2}>d_{2}
, we know there exists a
h
-length
s
-
t
path
P
with weight at most
\lambda_{2}
at the end of this phase, which means at the beginning of each of the
\Theta(h\log n/\delta_{0}^{2})
iterations in this phase,
P
has weight at most
\lambda_{2}=(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
. Then, in each of these iterations, because by the definition of path blockers, at least one edge
e\in P
will be saturated by
\hat{f}
, which means the weight of
e
increases by a factor
(1+\delta_{0})
. Because there are at most
h
edges on
P
(recall that edge lengths are positive integers), in this phase, there is an edge
e\in P
has its weight increasing by a factor of
(1+\delta_{0})^{\Theta(h\log n/\delta_{0}^{2})/h}
by an averaging argument. However, at the beginning of this phase, the weight of
e
is at least
1/m^{\zeta}
(since this is the initial weight), so at the end of this phase,
w(e)\geq(1/m^{1/\zeta})\cdot(1+\delta_{0})^{\Theta(h\log n/\delta_{0}^{2})/h}>m^{2}
by setting the constant hidden in
\Theta
to be large enough, and it contradicts that
P
has weight at most
\lambda_{2}=(1+\delta_{0})\lambda\leq 1+\delta_{0}
(the outter loop ensures
\lambda\leq 1
).

Next, we proof prove
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
at the end of each iteration. Again we use induction. Consider one iteration. Assume that
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
holds at the end of the previous iteration. Then this inequality holds at the beginning of this iteration, because
\lambda
may keep unchanged or increase. If
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)>(2+3\delta_{0})\lambda
at the beginning of this iteration, then actually we do nothing in this iteration because the path blocker
\hat{f}
will be a zero flow, so
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
holds at the end of this iteration trivially. Otherwise, we have
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})\lambda
at the beginning of this iteration. By our update rule of weights and the fact that
\hat{f}
is a feasible flow, the weight of each edge will only increase by at most a
1+\delta_{0}
factor in this iteration. Therefore, we also have
d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
at the end of this iteration.

∎

4.12
.

The pair
(f,w)
returned by
Algorithm

1
is feasible.

Proof.

The moving cut
w
is feasible because we have
\lambda\geq 1
and
\lambda\leq d^{(h)}_{w}(s,t)
at the end of the algorithm (by
4.11
). To see that the flow
f
is feasible, we let
\hat{F}=\sum\hat{f}=f/\eta
be the summation of path blockers without scaling. By our update rule, at the end, an edge
e
will have weight
w(e)=(1+\delta_{0})^{\hat{F}/U(e)}/m^{\zeta}
. Furthermore, we know that
w(e)\leq(1+\delta_{0})(2+3\delta_{0})
at the end because right before the last update of
w(e)
,
w(e)
must be at most
(2+3\delta_{0})\lambda
(otherwise, the path blocker
\hat{f}
cannot assign non-zero flow on
e
) and
w(e)
will increase by at most a
1+\delta_{0}
factor in this update. Therefore, from the inequality

(1+\delta_{0})^{\hat{F}/U(e)}/m^{\zeta}\leq(1+\delta_{0})(2+3\delta_{0}),

we have
\hat{F}(e)\leq U(e)\cdot(\log(1+\delta_{0})(2+3\delta_{0})+\log m/\delta_{0})/\log\zeta\leq U(e)\cdot(10\log m/\delta_{0}^{2})\leq U(e)/\eta
. Lastly, we have
f(e)=\eta\cdot\hat{F}(e)\leq U(e)
as desired.

∎

4.13
.

The pair
(f,w)
returned by
Algorithm

1
satisfies
|w|\leq O(\log n/\delta)\cdot\mathrm{val}(f)
.

Proof.

Again we use
\hat{F}=\sum\hat{f}
to denote the accumulated path blockers without scaling. Because
f=\eta\cdot\hat{F}
and
\eta=\Theta(\delta^{2}/\log m)
, it suffices to show that
|w|\leq O(\delta)\mathrm{val}(\hat{F})
.

We consider iterations one by one according to the time order. Fix an iteration, we then consider the flow paths in
\mathrm{path}(\hat{f})
one by one in some certain order. Our update rule on weights is equivalent to the following procedure: consider the flow paths in
\hat{F}
in the above order, and then for each path
P
, update the weights using
P
(i.e. for each
e\in P
, multiply
w(e)
by
(1+\delta_{0})^{\hat{f}(P)/U(e)}
).

Now we will show that, after updating the weights using some flow path
P
, the value of
|w|
increases by at most
O(\delta\hat{f}(P))
additively. To see this, note that at the beginning of this iteration, we have
w(P)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})\lambda
guaranteed by the path blocker. Let
w_{P}
denote the weight function right before the moment we update weights using
P
. We have
\sum_{e\in P}w_{P}(e)\leq(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
because each edge weight can increase by at most a
(1+\delta_{0})
factor in this iteration. Therefore, updating the weights using
P
will increase
|w|
by at most

\displaystyle~~~~\sum_{e\in P}(1+\delta_{0})^{\hat{f}(P)/U(e)}\cdot w_{P}(e)\cdot U(e)-\sum_{e\in P}w_{P}(e)\cdot U(e)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in P}(1+\delta_{0}\hat{f}(P)/U(e))\cdot w_{P}(e)\cdot U(e)-\sum_{e\in P}w_{P}(e)\cdot U(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in P}(\delta_{0}\hat{f}(P)/U(e))\cdot w_{P}(e)\cdot U(e)

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{0}(2+3\delta_{0})(1+\delta_{0})\lambda\cdot\hat{f}(P)

\displaystyle\leq O(\delta_{0}\hat{f}(P)).

Therefore, the final
|w|
is at most
\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(\hat{F})}O(\delta_{0})\hat{f}(P)=O(\delta)\mathrm{val}(\hat{F})
as desired.
∎

Time Analysis.

By
Corollary

4.10
, the running time for computing the blocker in one iteration is

t_{\mathrm{blocker}}=\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-\widetilde{\nabla}(v),0\}).

Recall that for each
v\in V\setminus\{s,t\}
,
\widetilde{\nabla}(v)=\nabla(v)
if currently
w(v,t)\leq(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
(or there is no such edge
e=(v,t)
), and
\widetilde{\nabla}(v)=0
if
w(v,t)>(1+\delta_{0})\cdot\lambda
. Thus, for each
v
with
w(v,t)>(1+\delta_{0}\lambda)
, we have

\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-\widetilde{\nabla}(v)\}=\deg_{G}^{+}(v)\leq\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-\nabla(v),0\}+\nabla(v).

Therefore, we can bound
t_{\mathrm{blocker}}
by

t_{\mathrm{blocker}}\leq\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\left(\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-\nabla(v),0\})+\sum_{v\text{ s.t. }w(v,t)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda}\nabla(v)\right).

4.14
.

For each edge
e
, if at some moment
w(e)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
holds, then finally we have
f(e)\geq\eta\cdot U(e)
.

Proof.

We have
w(e)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda\geq(1+\delta_{0})/m^{\zeta}
because
\lambda\geq 1/m^{\zeta}
. Combining the invariant
w(e)=(1+\delta_{0})^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}/m^{\zeta}
, we immediately get
f(e)\geq\eta\cdot U(e)
.
∎

Next, we bound the total sink capacities of vertices
v
s.t.
w(v,t)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda
by

\displaystyle\sum_{v\text{ s.t. }w(v,t)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda}\nabla(v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e=(v,t)\text{ s.t. }w(e)>(1+\delta_{0})\lambda}f(e)/\eta

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{val}(f)/\eta

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v)/\eta

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}\Delta(v),

where the first inequality is by
4.14
, the second inequality is because each flow path of
f
will go through exactly one sink edge, the third inequality is because the value of
f
is bounded by the total source capacities, and the last inequality is by
\eta=\Theta(\delta^{2}/\log m)
.

Lastly, we have

t_{\mathrm{blocker}}\leq\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\{s,t\}}(\Delta(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}^{+}(v)-\nabla(v),0\}),

and the final running time bound follows that the total number of iteration is at most
\mathrm{poly}(h,1/\delta,\log n)
.

4.3
Local Cutmatch: Proof of
Theorem

4.4

Providing the local length-constrained maxflow subroutine in
Theorem

4.3
, we are ready to prove
Theorem

4.4
. In this proof, When applying
Theorem

4.3
on some graph
H
, we will always set
\delta=0.01
. Our proof will exploit that the flow-cut pair
(f,w)
returned by
Theorem

4.3
is
O(\log n)
-approximate with additional properties stated in
Lemma

4.15
.

4.15
.

For
\delta=0.01
,
\zeta=1/(6\delta)+3
and
\eta=O(\delta^{2}/\log n)
. The output
(f,w)
given by
Theorem

4.3
satisfies the following.

1.

f/\eta
is an integral flow.

2.

For each edge
e\in E(H)
with
w(e)\geq 1/m
, there is
f(e)\geq\Omega_{\delta}(U(e))
.

3.

For each edge
e\in E(H)
with
w(e)\geq 1/m^{3}
, there is
f(e)>0
.

Proof.

Property (1) has been stated in
Theorem

4.3
. Properties (2) and (3) rely on the guarantee that, for each edge
e\in E(G)
,
w(e)=(1+\delta/6)^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}/m^{\zeta}
. Regarding property (2), for each edge
e
with
w(e)>1/m
, we have

\displaystyle(1+\delta/6)^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}/m^{\zeta}

\displaystyle\geq 1/m

\displaystyle(1+\delta/6)^{f(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}

\displaystyle\geq m

\displaystyle\frac{f(e)}{\eta\cdot U(e)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\log m}{\log(1+\delta/6)}

\displaystyle f(e)

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(U(e)).

Here the third inequality is because
\zeta>3
. For property (3), the invariant and
\zeta>3
immediately imply each edge
e
with
w(e)\geq 1/m^{3}>1/m^{\zeta}
has
f(e)>0
.
∎

The Algorithm.

We now show the local algorithm for computing cutmatch in
Theorem

4.4
. Recall that
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
is a matching between virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
.

We start with some notations. First, we define a directed auxiliary graph
H(A^{\mathrm{src}},A^{\mathrm{sink}})
, given source and sink node-weightings
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
.

1.

Start from the unit-capacitated directed version of
G
(i.e. substitute each undirected edge with two opposite directed edges with capacity
1
and the same length).

2.

Add a source vertex
s
and a sink vertex
t
.

3.

For each vertex
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}})
, add a
source edge

(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})
with length
\ell(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})=1
13
13

13

We set the lengths of source and sink edges to be
1
because the lengths should be positive integers.
and capacity
U(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})=A^{\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
. For each vertex
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}})
, add a
sink edge

(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)
with length
\ell(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)=1
and capacity
U(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)=A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})
.

We define some notations on
matchings
. For a matching
M
between two sets
V^{\mathrm{src}}
and
V^{\mathrm{sink}}
(
V^{\mathrm{src}}
and
V^{\mathrm{sink}}
may intersect), each edge
e\in E(M)
will connect a
source endpoint
in
V^{\mathrm{src}}
and
sink endpoint
in
V^{\mathrm{sink}}
, and its weight is
M(e)
if
M
is weighted. For each element
v\in V^{\mathrm{src}}
, we let
M^{\mathrm{src}}(v)
denote the total weights of matching edges with
v
as their source endpoint. Similarly, for each
v\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}
,
M^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)
denote the total weights of edges with
v
as their sink endpoint. Furthermore, we use
M^{\mathrm{src}}
(resp.
M^{\mathrm{sink}}
) refer to the set of elements in
V^{\mathrm{src}}
(resp.
V^{\mathrm{sink}}
) matched by
M
.

The cutmatch algorithm is iterative. We initialize the matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
to be empty. In the
i
-th iteration, we first define the remaining source and sink node-weightings
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
. Concretely, for each vertex
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}})
,
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v)=A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)-M^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)
14
14

14

Strictly speaking, we slightly abuse the notations here because
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
is between virtual nodes but
v
is a vertex. Here
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)
is naturally the sum of
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v_{\mathrm{vtul}})
over all
v
’s virtual nodes
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}
in
A^{\mathrm{src}}
.
. Similarly, for each vertex
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}})
,
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v)=A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)-M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)
. We run the local flow algorithm in
Theorem

4.3
on the graph
H_{i}=H(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
with
\delta=0.01
and length parameter
h=h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2
, and let the output be
(f_{i},w_{i})
. Let
\phi=\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}/\log^{2}n
. We now consider two cases.

The first case
\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\geq\phi|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|
.

In this case, we will compute a partial unweighted matching
M_{i}
between virtual nodes of
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
.

We first construct a fractional weighted matching
M^{\prime}_{i}
between vertices in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
. By our construction of
H_{i}
, each flow path
P_{H}\in\mathrm{path}(f_{i})
on
H_{i}
corresponds to a path
P
on
G
by removing the source and sink vertices
s
and
t
and the source and sink edges on
P
. Note that
P
now connects two vertices
v^{\mathrm{src}}
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}
on
G
. We add a matching edge with
v^{\mathrm{src}}
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}
as source and sink endpoints. The weight of this edge is
f_{i}(P_{H})
. Furthermore, we
simplify

M^{\prime}_{i}
to a simple graph. That is, for each pair of vertices
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
, if currently there are multiple matching edges connecting
v^{\mathrm{src}}
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}
in
M^{\prime}_{i}
, we merge them into a single matching edge with weight the total weight of these edges.

From the construction of
M^{\prime}_{i}
, for each vertex
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
,
M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
is at most the capacity of the source edge
(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})
, i.e.
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
. Because
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
is integral, we further have
\lceil M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\rceil\leq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
. Similarly, each vertex
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
has
\lceil M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{sink}}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})\rceil\leq A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})
. The size of
M^{\prime}_{i}
is bounded by
|M^{\prime}_{i}|\leq\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\leq\phi|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|
.

The next step is to round
M^{\prime}_{i}
into an integral weighted matching
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
between vertices in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
by applying
Lemma

4.16
.

4.16
[
KP15
]
.

Given a fractional matching
M
between two sets
V^{\mathrm{src}}
and
V^{\mathrm{sink}}
, there is an algorithm that rounds it to an integral matching
M_{{\rm int}}
s.t.

•

E(M_{{\rm int}})\subseteq E(M)
,
|M_{{\rm int}}|=\lceil|M|\rceil
;

•

each edge
e\in E(M_{{\rm int}})
has integral weight
M_{{\rm int}}(e)
s.t.
\lfloor M(e)\rfloor\leq M_{{\rm int}}(e)\leq\lceil M(e)\rceil

•

each
v\in V^{\mathrm{src}}
has
\lfloor M^{\mathrm{src}}(v)\rfloor\leq M_{{\rm int}}^{\mathrm{src}}(v)\leq\lceil M^{\mathrm{src}}(v)\rceil
and each
v\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}
has
\lfloor M^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)\rfloor\leq M_{{\rm int}}^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)\leq\lceil M^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)\rceil
.

The running time is
\tilde{O}(|E(M)|)
.

Finally, we
refine

M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
to be our unweighted matching
M_{i}
between virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
. That is, we first transform
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
into an unweighted matching by splitting each edge
e
into
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}(e)
many unweighted copies. Then for each vertex
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
, we assign each edge with
v
as its source endpoint to a distinct virtual node in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v)
. Note that because
M_{i}^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v)\leq\lceil M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v)\rceil\leq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v)
, there are enough virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v)
to receive matching edges of
v
. We do the similar things for each
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
.

We add
M_{i}
to
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
and proceed to the next iteration. The property of
M_{i}
summarized in
Lemma

4.17

4.17
.

The matching
M_{i}
can be embedded into
G
with congestion
O(1/\eta)
and length
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
. Furthermore,
|M_{i}|\geq\lceil\phi\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|\rceil
.

Proof.

We can bound the size of
M_{i}
by
|M_{i}|=|M^{\prime\prime}_{i}|=\lceil M^{\prime}_{i}\rceil\geq\lceil\phi\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|\rceil
.

For the embedding, we consider the flow
\hat{f}_{i}=(1/\eta)\cdot f_{i}
, where
\eta=\Theta(1/\log m)
is the parameter from
Lemma

4.15
. By (1) in
Lemma

4.15
,
\hat{f}_{i}
is an integral flow. Let
f_{i}^{G}
(resp.
\hat{f}_{i}^{G}
) be the flow on
G
by removing source edges and sink edges on flow paths of
f_{i}
(resp.
\hat{f}_{i}
).

Note that
f_{i}^{G}
and
\hat{f}_{i}^{G}
have congestion
2
and
2/\eta
respectively on
G
because each undirected unweighted edge on
G
corresponds to two directed edges with capacity
1
on
H_{i}
(from step 1 in the construction of
H(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
). Also,
f_{i}^{G}
and
\hat{f}_{i}^{G}
are
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length on
G
because
f_{i}
and
\hat{f}_{i}
are
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length on
H_{i}
and the source and sink edges removed have length
1
.

Now we construct an embedding of
M_{i}
into
G
. Because
M_{i}
is a refinement of
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, it is equivalent to consider
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
. For each matching edges
e=(v^{\mathrm{src}},v^{\mathrm{sink}})\in M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, we have
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}(e)\leq\lceil M^{\prime}_{i}(e)\rceil
. Furthermore, because
M^{\prime}_{i}(e)=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(f_{i}^{G})\text{ connecting $v^{\mathrm{src}}$ and $v^{\mathrm{sink}}$}}f_{i}^{G}(P)
and all
f^{G}_{i}(P)
are multiples of
\eta
, we have
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}(e)\leq\lceil M^{\prime}_{i}(e)\rceil\leq M^{\prime}_{i}(e)/\eta\leq\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(\hat{f}_{i}^{G})\text{ connecting $v^{\mathrm{src}}$ and $v^{\mathrm{sink}}$}}\hat{f}_{i}^{G}(P)
. Hence we can obtain an embedding
\Pi_{M_{i}\to G}
of
M_{i}
into
G
by decomposing each flow path
P
of
\hat{f}^{G}_{i}
into
\hat{f}^{G}_{i}(P)
many unweighted copies. The congestion and length of
\Pi_{M_{i}\to G}
are at most the congestion and length of
\hat{f}_{i}^{G}
respectively.

∎

The second case
\mathrm{val}(f_{i})<\phi|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|
.

This will be the last iteration of the algorithm. We will compute the last unweighted matching
M_{i}
between virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
, and finalize
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
by adding
M_{i}
. Also, we will compute the integral moving cut
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
and the partitions
(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
of
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
of
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
.

A natural idea to let
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
be the virtual nodes matched by
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
, and then compute
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
to
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separate
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
by rounding the fractional moving cut
h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot w_{i}
. Roughly speaking, each
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length path on
H_{i}
(which corresponding to an
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length path on
G
between
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
) will have total
w_{i}
-weight at least
1
, so multiplying
w_{i}
by
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
will bring
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
length increasing to this path, which gives the desired separation. However, a big issue of this idea is that,
w_{i}
may assign large cut value to the source and sink edges of
H_{i}
but these edges do not exist in
G
(so we call them
dummy edges
). This means some
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length path on
G
between
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
may not get enough length increase. This is the reason why we compute the last matching
M_{i}
. By further matching
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}
, we can somehow exclude all
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length paths with large cut value on dummy edges, where we heavily exploit the additional property (2) of
Theorem

4.3
.

Concretely, we consider another auxiliary graph
\bar{H}_{i}=H(x\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i},A^{\mathrm{sink}})
for some large constant
x
. Let
\bar{U}_{i}
be the capacity function of
\bar{H}_{i}
. We run the local flow algorithm from
Theorem

4.3
on
\bar{H}_{i}
with
\delta=0.01
and length parameter
h=h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2
to get
(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})
. Recall that property (2) in
Lemma

4.15
shows that each edge with non-negligible
\bar{w}_{i}
-weight (
\bar{w}(e)\geq 1/m
) will be almost saturated
(\bar{f}(e)\geq\Omega(\bar{U}_{i}(e)))
.

To construct
M_{i}
, we let
V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
collect all vertices
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
whose source edges have
\bar{w}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})\geq 1/m
, and let
V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
collect all vertices
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
s.t.
\bar{w}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)\geq 1/m
.
Similar to the first case, we transform
f_{i}
to a fractional matching
M^{\prime}_{i}
between vertices in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
. Each pair of vertices
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
has a matching edge with weight
\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(f_{i})\text{ connecting $v^{\mathrm{src}}$ and $v^{\mathrm{sink}}$}}f_{i}(P)
. Again, by applying
Lemma

4.16
, we round
M^{\prime}_{i}
to an integral matching
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
between vertices in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
. Next, we refine
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
to be a matching between virtual nodes (similar to the refinement operation in the first case), and let
M_{i}
be a submatching of the refined
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}
s.t. each vertex
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
has exactly
\min\{M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}),A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\}
many source virtual nodes matched by
M_{i}
.

4.18
.

For each vertex
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
, we have
M_{i}^{\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})=A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
.

Proof.

We first show
M_{i}^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\geq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
by

M_{i}^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\geq\lfloor M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor=\lfloor\bar{f}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor\geq\Omega(\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}}))=\Omega(x\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}))\geq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}),

where
M_{i}^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\geq\lfloor M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor
is by
Lemma

4.16
,
\lfloor M_{i}^{\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor=\lfloor\bar{f}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor
is from the construction of
M^{\prime}_{i}
,
\lfloor\bar{f}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})\rfloor\geq\Omega(\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}}))
is by
\bar{w}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})
and property (2) in
Lemma

4.15
, and
\Omega(x\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}))\geq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
is because we choose a sufficiently large constant
x
.

Therefore, by our construction of
M_{i}
, we have
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})=\min\{M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}}),A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\}=A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
.
∎

Finalizing
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

We add
M_{i}
to
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
, and
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
is now finalized.
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
can be embedded into
G
with congestion
\gamma_{\mathrm{CM}}=O(\log^{2}n/\phi)=O(\log^{4}n/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}})
and length
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
. The reason is that the number of iterations is
O(\log n/\phi)
by
Lemma

4.19
, and the matching
M_{i}
in each iteration can be embedded into
G
with congestion
O(\log n)
and length
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
by
Lemma

4.17
(it also holds for the
M_{i}
in the last iteration by the same proof of
Lemma

4.17
). Furthermore, this embedding
\Pi_{M_{\mathrm{CM}}\to G}
can be computed explicitly following the proof of
Lemma

4.17
.

4.19
.

The number of iterations is
O(\log n/\phi)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.17
, each first-case
i
-th iteration will add a matching
M_{i}
with size
|M_{i}|\geq\lceil\phi\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|\rceil
. Also, recall that the
i
-th iteration have

|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|=|A^{\mathrm{src}}|-\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq i-1}|M_{i^{\prime}}|=|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i-1}|-|M_{i-1}|\leq\lfloor(1-\phi)\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i-1}|\rfloor.

Thus the number of iterations is at most
\log_{1/(1-\phi)}\mathrm{poly}(n)=O(\log n/\phi)
.
∎

Partitions of
A^{\mathrm{src}}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
.

Let
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{CM}}
(resp.
M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}
) denote the set of all source (resp. sink) virtual nodes matched by
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
.
The set of source virtual nodes
A^{\mathrm{src}}
is partitioned into

A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}=M_{\mathrm{CM}}^{\mathrm{src}}\text{ and }A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}=A^{\mathrm{src}}\setminus M_{\mathrm{CM}}^{\mathrm{src}}.

Slightly differently,
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
is partitioned into

A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}=M_{\mathrm{CM}}^{\mathrm{sink}}\cup\bigcup_{v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}}A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})\text{ and }A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}=A^{\mathrm{sink}}\setminus A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}.

That is,
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
is the union of matched sink virtual node and all remaining sink virtual nodes of vertices in
V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
.

To see the correctness, we have
|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|\leq|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|
because the matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
match each virtual node in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
to a distinct virtual node in
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
. Next we show
|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|\leq O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|)
. Because
|M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}|=|M^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{CM}}|=|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|
, it suffices to argue that
\sum_{v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}}A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})\leq O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|)
. First, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}}A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}})=\sum_{v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}}\bar{U}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)\leq\sum_{v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}}O(\bar{f}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t))\leq O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})),

Note that for each
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\in V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
,
\bar{U}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)\leq O(\bar{f}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t))
by
\bar{w}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)>1/m
and property (2) in
Lemma

4.15
. We further have

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})

\displaystyle=|M^{\prime}_{i}|\leq|M^{\prime\prime}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})\text{
s.t. }\\
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\leq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\end{subarray}}M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})\text{
s.t. }\\
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})>A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\end{subarray}}\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})\text{
s.t. }\\
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\leq A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\end{subarray}}M^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})\text{
s.t. }\\
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})>A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\end{subarray}}O(M^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}))

\displaystyle=O(|M_{i}|)\leq O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|).

In the second line, we exploit that
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\leq\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})
for each
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
. In the third line, we exploit that, for each
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i})
s.t.
M^{\prime\prime,\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})>A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
,
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})=A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
by the construction of
M_{i}
, which implies
\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})\leq O(M^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}}))
combining
\bar{U}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})=x\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
.

The observation below is useful when constructing the moving cut
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

4.20
.

\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
is disjoint from
V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
, and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
is disjoint from
V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
.

Proof.

We have
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
is disjoint from
V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
because for each vertex
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
, virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}(v^{\mathrm{src}})\setminus A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
have been matched in the previous iterations, and virtual nodes in
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{src}})
are matched by
M_{i}
by
4.18
. Also
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
is disjoint from
V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
just by definition.
∎

Construction of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
.
For each edge
e\in E(G)
corresponding to two directed edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in H_{i}
, if
\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}\geq 1/m^{3}
(this threshold is from property (3) in
Lemma

4.15
), we let
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)=\lfloor 3h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}\rfloor
, otherwise
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)=0
.

Now we show the correctness of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
. Consider an arbitrary pair of vertices
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
and
v^{\mathrm{src}}\in\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
, and an
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length path
P_{G}
connecting them on
G
.
P_{G}
corresponds to a path
P_{H}=s\to v^{\mathrm{src}}\to\cdots P_{G}\cdots\to v^{\mathrm{sink}}\to t
on
\bar{H}_{i}
(by adding back the source and sink edges
(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})
and
(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)
) with length at most
h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2
, so
\bar{w}_{i}(P_{H})\geq 1
by the feasibility of
\bar{w}_{i}
. Because
v^{\mathrm{src}}\notin V^{\mathrm{src}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
and
v^{\mathrm{sink}}\notin V^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{sat}}
by
4.20
, we have
w^{\prime}_{i}(s,v^{\mathrm{src}})<1/m
and
w^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\mathrm{sink}},t)<1/m
. Therefore,

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P_{G})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{e\in P_{G}}\lfloor 3\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot(\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}-1/m^{3})\rfloor

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{e\in P_{G}}(3\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}-1-3h_{\mathrm{CM}}/m^{3})

\displaystyle\geq(3\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\sum_{e\in P_{G}}\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\})-(1+3h_{\mathrm{CM}}/m^{3})\cdot|E(P_{G})|

\displaystyle\geq 3h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot(1-2/m)-h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot(1+3/m^{2})

\displaystyle>h_{\mathrm{CM}}.

Here the first inequality is by the definition of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
. About the fourth inequality, note that

\sum_{e\in P_{G}}\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}\geq\bar{w}_{i}(P_{H})-2/m\geq 1-2/m

because the source and sink edges on
P_{H}
have
\bar{w}_{i}
-weight at most
1/m
. Moreover,

(1+3h_{\mathrm{CM}}/m^{3})\cdot|E(P_{G})|\leq|E(P_{G})|+3\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|E(P_{G})|/m^{3}\leq h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot(1+3/m^{2})

because
|E(P_{G})|\leq h_{\mathrm{CM}}
and
|E(P_{G})|\leq m
.

Furthermore,
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
has size

\displaystyle|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|

\displaystyle\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|\bar{w}_{i}|)\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot\log n)\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\cdot\log^{2}n)

\displaystyle\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\phi\cdot|A_{\mathrm{src}}|\cdot\log^{2}n)=O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A_{\mathrm{src}}|).

Here
|\bar{w}_{i}|\leq O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i}))
is by the approximation guarantee of the local flow algorithm on
\bar{H}_{i}
. Also,

\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\leq O(\mathrm{val}(f_{i}))
is by the following reasons. First,
\bar{f}_{i}
is a feasible
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length flow in
\bar{H}_{i}
, so it is a feasible
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length flow in
x\cdot H_{i}
(recall that
H_{i}=H(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
and
\bar{H}_{i}=H(x\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i})
). This means
\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})
is at most
x
times the value of maximum
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length flow in
H_{i}
. Hence
\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\leq O(x\cdot\mathrm{val}(f_{i}))=O(\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\log n)
because
f_{i}
is a
O(\log n)
-approximation
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}+2)
-length maxflow and
x
is a constant.

Construction of Landmarks.

We still use
i
to denote the last iteration of the local flow algorithm.

The first step is to construct a set
{\cal P}
of
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length paths on
G
that
covers

\mathrm{supp}(C_{\mathrm{CM}})
by taking all flow paths of
\bar{f}_{i}
with source and sink edges removed, where “cover” means that, for each edge
e\in E(G)
with
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)>0
, there exists a path
P\in{\cal P}
s.t.
e\in P
. The reason is as follows. Each edge
e\in E(G)
(corresponding to
e_{1},e_{2}\in\bar{H}_{i}
) with
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)>0
has
\max\{\bar{w}_{i}(e_{1}),\bar{w}_{i}(e_{2})\}\geq 1/m^{3}
, so either
\bar{f}_{i}(e_{1})>0
or
\bar{f}_{i}(e_{2})>0
by property (3) in
Lemma

4.15
, which implies
e\in P
for some
P\in{\cal P}
.

Next, we decompose paths in
{\cal P}
into
heavy edges
and
segments
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(3\cdot\kappa_{\sigma})
. Heavy edges are edges
e\in E({\cal P})
s.t.
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}
. Segments are constructed by processing each path
P\in{\cal P}
as follows. First, remove heavy edges from
P
. Second, for each remaining subpath
\widetilde{P}
, further decompose it into segments (i.e. shorter subpaths)
P^{\prime}
s.t.
\sigma^{\prime}\leq\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(P^{\prime})\leq 3\sigma^{\prime}
. This can be done by picking a prefix
P^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{P}
with
\sigma^{\prime}\leq\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(P^{\prime})\leq 2\sigma^{\prime}
repeatedly until the last subpath of
\widetilde{P}
has
(G-C_{\mathrm{CM}})
-length less than
\sigma^{\prime}
, and then concatenating the last subpath to its previous segment.

4.21
.

For some
\eta=\Theta(\log m)
, the total number of heavy edges is at most
O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\sigma^{\prime})
, and the total number of segments is at most
O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

First we have
|{\cal P}|\leq\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})/\eta
because each path
P_{H}\in\mathrm{supp}(\bar{f}_{i})
has
\bar{f}_{i}(P_{H})\geq\eta
by property (1) in
Lemma

4.15
. Again by this property, each edge in
G
will only appear in
O(1/\eta)
paths in
{\cal P}
, which means
\sum_{P\in{\cal P}}C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P)\leq O(|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\eta)
.

To bound the number of heavy edges, note that each heavy edge
e
has either
\ell_{G}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
or
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
because
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}
. For the heavy edges with
\ell_{G}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
, there are at most
O(|{\cal P}|\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\sigma^{\prime})
of them because each path in
{\cal P}
has length at most
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
on
G
. For the heavy edges with
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
, the number of them is obviously
O(|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\sigma^{\prime})
. Therefore, the total number of heavy edges is
O(|{\cal P}|\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\sigma^{\prime}+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\sigma^{\prime})=O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\sigma^{\prime})
.

We can bound the number of segments similarly. Each segment
P^{\prime}
has either
\ell_{G}(P^{\prime})\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
or
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P^{\prime})\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
. There are at most
O(|{\cal P}|\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\sigma^{\prime})
segments with
\ell_{G}(P^{\prime})\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
. The number of segments with
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P^{\prime})\geq\sigma^{\prime}/2
is at most
O(\sum_{P\in{\cal P}}C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P)/\sigma^{\prime})
. Thus the total number of segments is

O(|{\cal P}|\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\sigma^{\prime}+\sum_{P\in{\cal P}}C_{\mathrm{CM}}(P)/\sigma^{\prime})=O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})).

∎

Given the decomposition, we construct the landmark set
L
as follows. For each heavy edge
e=(u,v)
, we add both
u
and
v
into
L
(we say
u
and
v
are landmarks
created
by
e
). For each segment
P^{\prime}
, we add an arbitrary vertex
v\in P^{\prime}
into
L
(similarly,
v
is the landmark
created
by
P^{\prime}
). The size of
L
is linear to the number of heavy edges and segments, so

\displaystyle|L|

\displaystyle=O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/\sigma^{\prime})+O(\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime})+|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A_{\mathrm{src}}|\cdot\log^{2}n/(\eta\cdot\sigma^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq O(\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot\kappa_{\sigma}),

Here the first inequality is because
\mathrm{val}(\bar{f}_{i})\leq O(x\cdot\mathrm{val}(f_{i}))\leq O(\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\log n)
(we have shown this when bounding
|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|
). Moreover, recall that
\mathrm{val}(f_{i})\leq\phi\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|=\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}|/\log^{2}n
by the condition of the second case, and
|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq O(h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A_{\mathrm{src}}|)
. The last inequality is because
\eta=\Theta(\log n)
and
\sigma^{\prime}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}/(3\cdot\kappa_{\sigma})
.

L
is a landmark set of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
on
G
with distortion
\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}=3\sigma^{\prime}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}/\kappa_{\sigma}
by the following reasons. All edges
e\in E(G)
with
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(e)>\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}
are heavy edges, so their endpoints are landmarks. For those
e\in E(G)
with
C_{\mathrm{CM}}(e)>0
and
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}(e)\leq\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}
, it must belong to a segment (or it is a heavy edge itself), so the landmark created by this segment (or this heavy edge) has
(G-C_{\mathrm{CM}})
-distance at most
3\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}
to endpoints of
e
.

Time Analysis.

The running time is dominated by the local flow subroutine of each iteration. For iteration
i
, the local flow subroutine will be run on graph
H_{i}
s.t. for each
v\in V(G)
,
\deg_{H_{i}}^{+}(v)\leq\deg_{G}(v)+2
(because each vertex may incident to the source edge and sink edge added to
H_{i}
),
\Delta_{H_{i}}(v)=A^{\mathrm{src}}_{i}(v)\leq A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)
and
\nabla_{H_{i}}(v)=A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{i}(v)\geq A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)-M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)
.
By
Theorem

4.3
, the running time of local flow on
H_{i}
is (pick parameter
c=3
)

\displaystyle~~~~\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(H_{i})\setminus\{s,t\}}(3\cdot\Delta_{H_{i}}(v)+\max\{\deg^{+}_{H_{i}}(v)-3\cdot\nabla_{H_{i}}(v),0\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)}(3\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}(v)+2-3\cdot(A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)-M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)),0\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot

\displaystyle~~~~\sum_{v\in V(G)}(3\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+\max\{\deg_{G}(v)+2-3\cdot(A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)+A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)),0\}+3\cdot A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+3\cdot M_{\mathrm{CM}}^{\mathrm{sink}}(v))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot(9\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|+\sum_{v\in V(G)}\max\{\deg_{G}(v)-(A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)),0\})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|.

The third inequality is because
\sum_{v\in V(G)}M^{\mathrm{sink}}_{\mathrm{CM}}(v)=|M_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq|A^{\mathrm{src}}|
and
A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)\geq 1
and the last inequality is by
A^{\mathrm{src}}(v)+A^{\mathrm{sink}}(v)\geq\deg_{G}(v)
for all
v\in V(G)
. The last iteration will run one more local flow subroutine on graph
\bar{H}_{i}
. The running time bound is the same by the same analysis. Finally, the total running time is
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}},\log n)\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}
because the number of iterations is
O(\log n/\phi)=O(\log^{3}/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}})
by
Lemma

4.19
.

5
Dynamic Routers with Path Reporting

In this section, we introduce a dynamic router algorithm, which is a key tool of the dynamic length-constrained expander decomposition in
Sections

6
and
7
. As we mentioned in
Section

3.3
, the number of batched updates received by a router (denoted by
t_{\mathrm{local}}
in
Theorem

5.1
) might not be the same with the global time parameter
t
. We now specify the relations between
t_{\mathrm{local}}
and
t
. In fact, when we apply
Theorem

6.4
in
Section

6
, we have
t_{\mathrm{local}}=O(t)=O(1/\epsilon)
. When we apply
Theorem

5.1
in
Section

7
, we have
t_{\mathrm{local}}=O(t\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{insLM},t})=O(1/\epsilon^{5})
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{insLM},t}=O(1/\epsilon^{4})
is from
Lemma

7.11
. To avoid clutter, we manually set an upper bound
t_{\mathrm{local}}=\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)
in the statement of
Theorem

5.1
.

5.1
Dynamic Routers.

Given a set
V
, there is an algorithm that initializes a
(h^{(0)}_{\mathrm{rt}},\gamma^{(0)}_{rt})
