-router
R^{(0)}
with
V(R^{(0)})=V
for node-weighting
\mathds{1}(V)
with
h^{(0)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(0)
,
\gamma^{(0)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\gamma}(0)
and maximum degree
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(i)=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}+O(i)
and
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\gamma}(i)=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4}+i)}
are global parameters (with respect to
i
). For some
t_{\mathrm{local}}=\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)
, the algorithm can also maintain
R
under a sequence
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)},...,\pi^{(t_{\mathrm{local}})}
of the following batched updates.

•

(Edge Deletion) If
\pi_{i}=E_{F}\subseteq E(R^{(i-1)})
denotes edge deletions, the algorithm computes a set of pruned vertices
V_{{\rm prune}}\subseteq V(R^{(i-1)})
and a
(h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}},\gamma^{(i)}_{rt})
-router
R^{(i)}
which is the subgraph of
R^{(i-1)}
induced by
V(R^{(i-1)})\setminus V_{{\rm prune}}
s.t.
R^{(i)}
has no edge in
E_{F}
,

h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(i),\ \gamma^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\gamma}(i)\text{ and }|V_{{\rm prune}}|=\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(i)\cdot|\pi_{i}|

where
\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(i)=t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}=(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}
.

•

(Matching Insertion) If
\pi_{i}=M
denotes a matching between
V(R^{(i-1)})
and a set of new vertices
V_{{\rm new}}
, the new router
R^{(i)}=R^{(i-1)}\cup M
(in particular,
V(R^{(i)})=V(R^{(i-1)})\cup V_{{\rm new}}
) is a
(h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}},\gamma^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}})
-router with

h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(i),\text{ and }\gamma^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\gamma}(i).

The initialization time is
|V(R^{(0)})|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
and the update time for
\pi^{(i)}
is
t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|=(1/\epsilon)^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|
.

Furthermore, at any moment
0\leq i\leq t
, given two vertices
u,v\in V(R^{(i)})
, there is an algorithm that outputs a path
P
connecting
u
and
v
in
R^{(i)}
with length at most
h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(i)
in time
O(|P|/\epsilon^{4})
.

Proof.

At any time
0\leq i\leq t
, the router
R^{(i)}
is always the union of a
core graph

J^{(i)}
and an
affiliated forest

{\cal T}^{(i)}
. Vertices in
V(J^{(i)})
are
core vertices
and other vertices in
V(R^{(i)})\setminus V(J^{(i)})
are
affiliated vertices
. Each core vertex
v
one-one corresponds to an
affiliated tree

T_{v}\in{\cal T}^{(i)}
, which means each tree
T_{v}\in{\cal T}^{(i)}
only contains one core vertex
v
and the other vertices in
T_{v}
are affiliated vertices. Although the tree edges are undirected, we manually set the cored vertex
v
to be the root of
T_{v}
to establish the notions of ancestors and descendants on affiliated trees. Let
\tau^{(i)}
denote the maximum tree size in
{\cal T}^{(i)}
.

Initialization.

We first introduce a recursive partition of
V
called the
group hierarchy
. Let
b=\Theta(n^{\epsilon^{4}})
be an integral parameter only used in this proof. We decompose the vertex set
V
into
groups
(i.e. subsets of vertices) recursively. We use
{\cal B}^{(0)}
to denote the initial set of all groups and use
{\cal B}^{(0)}_{j}
to denote the initial set of groups at level
j
, and the first level only has a single group containing all vertices
V
, i.e.
{\cal B}^{(0)}_{1}=\{V\}
. For each level
j
and each group
B\in{\cal B}^{(0)}_{j}
with size
|B|\geq 10\cdot b
, we partition
B
into
b
subgroups, each of which has size between
\lfloor|B|/b\rfloor
and
\lceil|B|/b\rceil
, and then we add these subgroups into level
j+1
. We call these subgroups the
children
of
B
and we call
B
the
parent
of them. Observe that the groups form a tree structure. Furthermore, the number of levels is at most
O(\log_{b}|V|)=O(1/\epsilon^{4})
.

Initially, the core graph
J^{(0)}
has vertices
V(J^{(0)})=V
, and we construct the edges
E(J^{(0)})
based on the groups. Concretely, consider each group
B\in{\cal B}
. If
B
has no children (namely,
B
is a leaf in the hierarchy), we add a clique among vertices in
B
. If
B
has children, for each pair of its children
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, we add a matching
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}
between
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
with size
\min\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\}
. Recall that
|B_{1}|
and
|B_{2}|
differ by at most one, so at most one vertex in
B_{1}
or
B_{2}
is not incident to this matching. The edge set
E(J^{(0)})
is the union of the cliques and matchings mentioned above.

We set the initial router
R^{(0)}=J^{(0)}
. Namely, there is no affiliated vertices and each affiliated tree contains just a single core vertex.

The maximum degree of
R^{(0)}
is
n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
by the following reasons. For each vertex
v\in V(R^{(0)})
and each group
B
containing
v
, the clique or matchings added by
B
will bring at most
b
incident edges to
v
. Because the number of group
B
containing
v
is at most
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
(i.e. the number of levels), we have
\deg_{R^{(0)}}(v)\leq O(1/\epsilon^{4})\cdot b\leq n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
.

The initialization time is obviously proportional to the graph size, i.e.
|R^{(0)}|=|V|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
.

The Edge Deletion Update.

When
\pi^{(i)}=E_{F}\subseteq E(R^{(i-1)})
is a batch of edge deletions, we will compute the set
V_{{\rm prune}}
of pruned vertices as follows. We let
V_{\mathrm{aff}}=V(E_{F})
collect all endpoints of deleted edges, called
affected vertices
. We then call
V_{\mathrm{aff},J}=V_{\mathrm{aff}}\cap V(J^{(i-1)})

affected core vertices
and naturally
V_{\mathrm{aff},{\cal T}}=V_{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus V(J^{(j-1)})
is the set of
affected affiliated vertices
.

We construct the set
V_{{\rm prune},J}
of
pruned core vertices
by the following procedure. At the beginning, we let
V_{{\rm prune},J}=V_{\mathrm{aff},J}
. Then, we iterate
j
from the maximum level to the first level of
{\cal B}^{(i-1)}
. For each group
B\in{\cal B}^{(i-1)}_{j}
, if
|B\cap V_{{\rm prune},J}|\geq|B|/(100t_{\mathrm{local}})
, we add the whole group
B
into
V_{{\rm prune},J}
. Finally, the new group hierarchy
{\cal B}^{(i)}
is obtained by dropping from
{\cal B}^{(i-1)}
all groups added into
V_{{\rm prune},J}
.

The whole set
V_{{\rm prune}}
of pruned vertices contains all pruned core vertices
V_{{\rm prune},J}
, all affected affiliated vertices
V_{\mathrm{aff},{\cal T}}
, and the descendants of
V_{{\rm prune},J}\cup V_{\mathrm{aff},{\cal T}}
in the affiliated forest.

The Size of
V_{{\rm prune}}
.

We first bound the number of pruned core vertices (i.e.
|V_{{\rm prune},J}|
) as follows. Recall the procedure constructing
V_{{\rm prune},J}
. At the beginning of this procedure,
|V_{{\rm prune},J}|=|V_{\mathrm{aff},J}|\leq|V_{\mathrm{aff}}|\leq 2|E_{F}|
. Then the procedure goes from the maximum level to the first level. At each level
j
and each group
B\in{\cal B}_{j}^{(i-1)}
, we will add the whole group
B
into
V_{{\rm prune},J}
if currently
|B\cap V_{{\rm prune},J}|\geq|B|/(100t)
. Because the groups at level
j
are disjoint,
|V_{{\rm prune},J}|
will blow up by a factor of at most
100t
after processing level
j
. Finally, we have
|V_{{\rm prune},J}|\leq(100t_{\mathrm{local}})^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}|E_{F}|\leq t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}|E_{F}|
because the number of levels is at most
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
.

Next, we can easily bound the number of pruned vertices by
|V_{{\rm prune}}|\leq(|V_{\mathrm{aff},{\cal T}}|+|V_{{\rm prune},J}|)\cdot\tau^{(i)}\leq t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}\cdot|E_{F}|
. Here
\tau^{(i)}
is the maximum size of affiliated trees in
{\cal T}^{(i)}
(trivially
\tau^{(i)}\leq 2^{i}
). The first inequality is because each affected affiliated vertex and pruned core vertex has at most
\tau^{(i)}
descendants.

The Matching Insertion Update.

When
\pi^{(i)}=M
is a matching between new vertices
V_{{\rm new}}
and a subset of old vertices, we let all new vertices be affiliated vertices, and keep the core graph
J^{(i)}=J^{(i-1)}
unchanged (also
{\cal B}^{(i)}={\cal B}^{(i-1)}
). Then we add these new affiliated vertices to the affiliated forest, i.e.
{\cal T}^{(i)}={\cal T}^{(i-1)}\cup M
. In other words, we just update
R^{(i)}
to be
R^{(i-1)}\cup M
.

The Update Time.

The matching insertion update obviously takes
O(|\pi^{(i)}|)
time. The edge deletion update time can be upper bounded by
O(|V_{{\rm prune},J}|/\epsilon^{4})
because at each level we just need to scan the current
V_{{\rm prune},J}
, and the number of levels is at most
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
. We have shown
|V_{{\rm prune},J}|\leq t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|
and the number of levels is
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
, so the update time is
V_{{\rm prune},J}\cdot O(1/\epsilon^{4})=t_{\mathrm{local}}^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}\cdot 2^{O(i)}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|
.

The Quality of the Router.

We now show that
R^{(i)}
is an
(h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}},\gamma^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}})
-router for node-weighting
\mathds{1}(V)
with
h^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}+O(i)
and
\gamma^{(i)}_{\mathrm{rt}}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4}+i)}
. Before proving this, we first show that each group
B\in B^{(i)}
only has a small fraction of pruned vertices (i.e.
5.2
), which implies a matching between two surviving groups still has a large fraction of surviving matching edges (i.e.
5.3
).

5.2
.

For each group
B\in B^{(i)}
,
|B\setminus V(J^{(i)})|\leq 0.01|B|
.

Proof.

Because
B\in B^{(i)}
, by our update algorithm,
B
is not dropped in all edge deletion update so far, which means at most
|B|/(100t)
many vertices are pruned in each previous edge deletion update. Therefore, the total number of pruned vertices in
B
is at most
i\cdot|B|/(100t)\leq 0.01|B|
.
∎

5.3
.

For each pair of children
(B_{1},B_{2})
of some group
B\in{\cal B}^{(i)}
,
|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|\geq 0.8\max\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\}
.

Proof.

By
5.2
, we can show that
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}
still has large fraction of surviving matching edges, i.e.

\displaystyle|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|

\displaystyle\geq|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}|-\max\{|B_{1}\setminus V(J^{(i)})|,|B_{2}\setminus V(J^{(i)})|\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\}-0.01\max\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\}

\displaystyle\geq 0.8\max\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\},

where the last inequality is because
|B_{1}|
and
|B_{2}|
differ by at most 1 and
|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\geq 10
(since
B
has children which implies
|B|\geq 10b
).
∎

5.4
.

An arbitrary
\mathds{1}(V(J^{(i)}))
-respecting demand can be routed on the core graph
J^{(i)}
with length
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}
and congestion
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}
.

Proof.

We will show this by induction. Let
\bar{j}=O(1/\epsilon^{4})
be the number of levels. Let the induction hypothesis at level
j
be that, for each group
B\in{\cal B}^{(i)}_{j}
, an arbitrary
\mathds{1}(V(J^{(i)})\cap B)
-respecting demand can be routed on the subgraph
J^{(i)}\cap B
with length
4^{\bar{j}-j+1}
and congestion
4^{\bar{j}-j+1}
. In the base case when
j
is the maximum level, the hypothesis trivially holds because each group
B
at the maximum level is a leaf in the hierarchy, which means the subgraph
J^{(i)}\cap B
contains a clique on vertices
V(R^{(i)})\cap B
.

Consider an arbitrary level
j
and assume the hypothesis holds for level
j+1
. For each group
B\in{\cal B}^{(i)}_{j}
, if
B
is a leaf, then the hypothesis holds for
B
by the same reason. Otherwise,
B
has several children in
B^{(i-1)}
, and all surviving vertices
V(J^{(i)})\cap B
are contained in these children. Let
D
be an arbitrary
\mathds{1}(V(J^{(i)})\cap B)
-respecting demand on
V(J^{(i)})\cap B
. We decompose
D
into inner-children demands and inter-children demands, i.e.

D=\sum_{\text{children }B_{1}\text{ of }B}D_{\mid B_{1}}+\sum_{\text{children pairs }(B_{1},B_{2})}D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})},

where
D_{\mid B_{1}}
collect all demand pairs of
D
with both endpoints in
B_{1}
, and
D_{B_{1},B_{2}}
collect demand pairs with one endpoint in
B_{1}
and the other one in
B_{2}
.

For each inner-children demand
D_{\mid B_{1}}
, the hypothesis tells that
D_{\mid B_{1}}
can be routed on
J^{(i)}\cap B_{1}
with length
4^{\bar{j}-j}
and congestion
4^{\bar{j}-j}
. Because the children of
B
are disjoint, routing all inner-children demands
\sum D_{\mid B_{1}}
has length
4^{\bar{j}-j}
and congestion
4^{\bar{j}-j}
on
J^{(i)}\cap B
.

For each inter-children demand
D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}
and each demand pair
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathrm{supp}(D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})})
(s.t.
u_{1}\in B_{1}
and
u_{2}\in B_{2}
), we will route it through the surviving matching edges in
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}
evenly. Concretely, for each
e=(w_{1},w_{2})\in M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}
(where
w_{1}\in B_{1}
and
w_{2}\in B_{2}
), we will route
D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}(u_{1},u_{2})/|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|
units of demand through
e
, and to complete the routing path, we will request the children
B_{1},B_{2}
to route
D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}(u_{1},u_{2})/|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|
units from
u_{1}
to
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
to
u_{2}
in
J^{(i)}\cap B_{1}
and
J^{(i)}\cap B_{2}
respectively.

Now we show the length and congestion of the above routing of the total inter-children demand
\sum D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}
on
J^{(i)}\cap B
. For each matching
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}
, the congestion of each surviving matching edge can be bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathrm{supp}(D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})})}D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}(u_{1},u_{2})/|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|\leq\min\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\}/(0.8\max\{|B_{1}|,|B_{2}|\})\leq 2,

where the first inequality uses
5.3
.
For each child
B_{1}
and each surviving vertex
v_{1}\in J^{(i)}\cap B_{1}
, the newly requested inner-children demand involving
v_{1}
can be bounded as follows. When
v
acts as
u_{1}
, the contribution is at most

\sum_{\text{children }B_{2}\text{ of }B}\ \sum_{v_{2}\in B_{2}\cap V(J^{(i)})}D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}(v_{1},v_{2})\leq\sum_{v_{2}\in(B\setminus B_{1})\cap V(J^{(i)})}D(v_{1},v_{2})\leq 1

because
D
is a
\mathds{1}(B\cap V(J^{(i)}))
-respecting demand. When
v_{1}
acts as
w_{1}
(i.e.
v_{1}
incident to some surviving matching edges), the contribution is at most

\displaystyle~~~~\sum_{\text{children }B_{2}\text{ of }B}\ \sum_{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathrm{supp}(D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})})}D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}(u_{1},u_{2})/|M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u_{1}\in B_{1}\cap V(J^{(i)})}\ \sum_{u_{2}\in(B\setminus B_{1})\cap V(J^{(i)})}D(u_{1},u_{2})/(0.8|B_{1}|)

\displaystyle\leq 2.

In the first-line expression, the first summation is enumerating the possible surviving matching edges incident to
v
(at most one for each other children
B_{2}
), and the second summation is bounding the demands routed through this edge. The first inequality use
5.3
. The above argument shows that the newly request inner-children demand for each child
B_{1}
is
3\cdot\mathds{1}(B_{1}\cap V(J^{(i)}))
-respecting, so it can be routed on
J^{(i)}\cap B_{1}
with length
4^{\bar{j}-j}
and congestion
3\cdot 4^{\bar{j}-j}
. Finally, concatenate the routing on surviving matching edges and the routing of newly requested inner-children demands, the inter-children demand
\sum D_{\mid(B_{1},B_{2})}
can be routed on
J^{(i)}\cap B
with length
2\cdot 4^{\bar{j}-j}+1\leq 4^{\bar{j}-j+1}
and congestion
3\cdot 4^{\bar{j}-j}
.

Combining two parts,
D
can be routed on
B\cap J^{(i)}
with length
4^{\bar{j}-j+1}
and congestion
(1+3)\cdot 4^{\bar{j}-j}=4^{\bar{j}-j+1}
as desired.

∎

We now ready to show the quality of the router. Recall that
\tau^{(i)}
denote the maximum size of trees in
{\cal T}^{(i)}
, and trivially we have
\tau^{(i)}\leq 2^{i}
. Consider an arbitrary
\mathds{1}(V(R^{(i)}))
-respecting demand
D
. We will route
D
in two phases. In the first phase, we route all demands on affiliated vertices to core vertices along the affiliated trees, which will cause length
h_{\cal T}=i
and congestion
\gamma_{\cal T}=\tau^{(i)}=2^{i}
because the affiliated trees in
{\cal T}^{(i)}
have maximum depth
i
. The first phase will reduce the original demand
D
to a new demand
D_{\mathrm{core}}
on core vertices. Note that
D_{\mathrm{core}}
is a
\tau^{(i)}\cdot\mathds{1}(V(J^{(i)}))
-respecting demand on
J^{(i)}
. In the second phase, we route the demand
D_{\mathrm{core}}
on the core graph
J^{(i)}
using
Lemma

5.4
, which has length
h_{J}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}
and congestion
\gamma_{J}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4}+i)}
. By concatenating the routings in two phases, we can obtain a routing of
D
on
R^{(i)}
with length
h_{\mathrm{rt}}=2\cdot h_{\cal T}+h_{J}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}+O(i)
and congestion
\gamma_{\mathrm{rt}}=\gamma_{\cal T}+\gamma_{J}=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4}+i)}
.

The Path Reporting Query.

Finally, we describe the path reporting query algorithm. Let
u,v\in V(R^{(i)})
be the input vertices. Let
u_{J},v_{J}
be the roots of the affiliated trees containing
u
and
v
. The path between
u
and
u_{J}
and the path between
v
and
v_{J}
can be easily outputted by working on the affiliated trees, so it remains to output a path connecting
u_{J}
and
v_{J}
on
J^{(i)}
.

The idea to output a path connecting
u_{J}
and
v_{J}
is similar (but simpler) to the proof of the quality of the router. The algorithm is recursive. In each recursive step, our input is a group
B
and two vertices
u_{1},u_{2}\in B\cap V(J^{(i)})
(the input of the initial recursive step is
u_{J},v_{J}
and the unique group
B=V\in{\cal B}^{(i)}_{1}
, which obviously satisfies the requirement), and the task is to output a path connecting
u_{1}
and
v_{2}
in
B\cap J^{(i)}
. If
B
is a leaf in the group hierarchy, there is a clique on
B\cap V(J^{(i)})
, so we can just return the edge connecting
u_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Otherwise,
B
has several children. If there is a child
B_{1}
of
B
containing both
u_{1},u_{2}
, it suffices to invoke a recursive step with input
(B_{1},u_{1},v_{2})
. From now suppose
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are inside two different children
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
. We pick an arbitrary surviving matching edge
e=(w_{1},w_{2})\in M_{B_{1},B_{2}}\cap J^{(i)}
(s.t.
w_{1}\in B_{1}
and
w_{2}\in B_{2}
). Then we invoke two recursive steps with inputs
(B_{1},u_{1},w_{1})
and
(B_{2},w_{2},u_{2})
respectively, which output two paths from
u_{1}
to
w_{1}
and from
w_{2}
to
u_{2}
. Concatenating these two paths and the matching edge
e
, we obtain a path connecting
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
in
B\cap J^{(i)}
.

The above algorithm compute a path connecting
u_{J}
and
v_{J}
on
J^{(i)}
with length
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}
, because the group hierarchy has at most
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
levels. Hence finally we obtain a path connecting
u
and
v
with length at most
2^{O(1/\epsilon^{4})}+O(i)
. The path-reporting time is
O(|P|/\epsilon^{4})
because finding each edge takes time proportional to the number of levels.

∎

6
Dynamic Length-Constrained Expander Decomposition

In this section, we establish an algorithm to maintain a (weak) length-constrained expander decomposition
C
on an online-batch dynamic graph
G
. In fact, instead of just the integral moving cut
C
, we will maintain an object called
certified expander decomposition
(see
Definition

6.1
), which is slightly different with the notion of witnessed-ED in
Definition

3.17
. The main theorem of this section is
Theorem

6.4
.

6.1
Certified Expander Decomposition.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A
. A
certified expander decomposition
(
certified-ED
for short) of
A
on
G
is denoted by
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
where

•

C
is an integral moving cut on
G
.

•

L
is a landmark set of
C
on
G
with distortion
\sigma
.

•

{\cal N}
denotes a
b
-distributed
(h_{{\rm cov}},h_{\mathrm{sep}},\omega)
-neighborhood cover of
A
on
G-C
with
h_{{\rm cov}}=h_{\mathrm{sep}}=h
.

•

{\cal R}=\{R^{S}\mid S\in{\cal N}\}
is a collection of routers s.t. for each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
,
R^{S}
is a router with
V(R^{S})\supseteq S
, and it is maintained by
Theorem

5.1
under at most
f
update batches. In particular, each vertex in
V(R^{S})
is either
redundant
or corresponding to a distinct virtual node in
S
.

•

\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
denotes an embedding of all routers (i.e.
\bigcup_{R\in{\cal R}}R
) into
G
with length
h_{{\rm emb}}
and congestion
\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
. In this embedding, each router vertex
v_{R}
is mapped to the vertex
v_{G}\in V(G)
which owns the corresponding virtual node of
v_{R}
(if
v_{R}
is a redundant vertex, then it can be mapped to an arbitrary vertex
v_{G}\in V(G)
).

The parameters
\sigma,h_{\mathrm{trace}},\gamma_{\mathrm{trace}},b,h,\omega,f,h_{{\rm emb}},\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
are called
quality parameters
, which are part of the parameters of the certified expander decomposition, and we will store them explicitly. In particular, we call
h
the
length parameter
of this certified-ED. The theorem statements in this section will refer to these parameters. We omit them in the tuple
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
just to avoid clutter.

By exploiting elementary data structures like binary search trees, all accesses to certified-EDs in our update algorithm can be done in polylogarithmic time, which is negligible because we allow
n^{O(\epsilon)}
overhead in the update time. However, in
Section

10.1
we will design access interfaces with constant access time, in order to achieve
sublogarithmic query time.

The main differences between our certified-ED and the witnessed-ED in
Definition

3.17
are as follows. First, the clusters in our neighborhood cover
are sets of virtual nodes in
A
rather than vertices in
V(G)
. Second, the routers in certified-EDs have router vertex corresponding to virtual nodes, and they are instances of our dynamic router algorithm
Theorem

5.1
. Third, we store an additional landmark set of
C
, which is for reducing the recourse.

For better understanding, we point out that a certified-ED essentially implies some kind of flow characterization of the graph
G
with moving cut
C
by
Lemma

6.2
. However, Strictly speaking,
C
is
not
a length-constrained expander decomposition of
G
, because the flow characterization from
Lemma

6.2
cannot guarantee that
G-C
is a length-constrained expander. Precisely,
Lemma

6.2
only talks about routability on
G
, while a length-constrained expander decomposition
C
requires routability on
G-C
. Nonetheless, this
weak
form of length-constrained expander decomposition is sufficient in our scenario, because indeed we only need short paths on
G
(not
G-C
) to certify pairwise distances. In fact, we only require the length guarantee from
Lemma

6.3
to obtain distance approximations, and the congestion guarantee of the embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
is purely for fast update time.

6.2
.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A
. If there exists a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
, then any
h
-length
A
-respecting demand on
G-C
can be routed on
G
with length
\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(f)\cdot h_{{\rm emb}}
and congestion
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\gamma}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
.

The proof of
Lemma

6.2
is quite similar to the proof of
Lemma

3.16
, so we omit it here.

6.3
.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A
and a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
. For any pair of vertices
u,v\in V(G)
s.t. there exists a cluster
S\in{\cal N}
with
u,v\in\mathrm{supp}(S)
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(f)\cdot h_{{\rm emb}}
.

Proof.

This is because each router has diameter at most
\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},h}(f)
and it can be embedded into
G
with length
h_{{\rm emb}}
.
∎

Theorem

6.4
concludes our online-batch dynamic algorithm for a dynamic graph
G
with a dynamic node-weighting
A
. We note that the batched updates for
(G,A)
are defined slightly different from those just for a graph. Precisely, in
Theorem

6.4
, each batched update
\pi^{(i)}
contains five types of unit updates: edge insertions/deletions, isolated vertex insertions/deletions and node-weighting insertions. Without loss of generality, we assume the unit updates in
\pi^{(i)}
have the same type.

•

When
\pi^{(i)}
represents batched edge insertions, we are given an edge set
E_{{\rm new}}
, which updates
E(G^{(i)})=E(G^{(i-1)})\cup E_{{\rm new}}
, and we say
\pi^{(i)}
has size
|\pi^{(i)}|=|E_{{\rm new}}|
.

•

When
\pi^{(i)}
represents batched edge deletions, we are given an edge set
F\subseteq E(G^{(i-1)})
, which updates
E(G^{(i)})=E(G^{(i-1)})\setminus F
. The size is
|\pi^{(i)}|=|F|
.

•

When
\pi^{(i)}
represents batched isolated vertex insertions, the input is a vertex set
V_{{\rm new}}
with a node-weighting
A_{{\rm new}}
on it, which updates
V(G^{(i)})=V(G^{(i-1)})\cup V_{{\rm new}}
and
A^{(i)}=A^{(i-1)}\uplus A_{{\rm new}}
.
15
15

15

Recall the definition of operation
\uplus
from
Section

3.4
.
The size is
|\pi^{(i)}|=\sum_{v\in V_{{\rm new}}}A_{{\rm new}}(v)
.

•

When
\pi^{(i)}
represents batched isolated vertex deletions, the input is a set
V_{\mathrm{del}}\subseteq V(G^{(i-1)})
of isolated vertices on
G^{(i-1)}
, which updates
V(G^{(i)})=V(G^{(i-1)})\setminus V_{{\rm new}}
and
A^{(i)}=(A^{(i-1)})_{\mid V(G^{(i)})}
. The size is
|\pi^{(i)}|=\sum_{v\in V_{\mathrm{del}}}A^{(i-1)}(v)
.

•

When
\pi^{(i)}
represents batched node-weighting insertions, the input is a node-weighting
A_{{\rm new}}
on
V(G^{(i-1)})
, which updates
A^{(i)}=A^{(i-1)}+A_{{\rm new}}
. The size is
\pi^{(i)}=|A_{{\rm new}}|
.

Particularly, a batched isolated vertex insertions/deletions update of
(G,A)
has size depending on the node weighting. However, when we apply
Theorem

6.4
in the following sessions, the node-weighting
A
is actually always bounded by a constant, so the size of batched isolated vertex insertions/deletions will still be linear to the size of vertex set as usual.

6.4
.

Let
G
be a dynamic graph with an incremental node-weighting
A
s.t.
A^{(0)}\geq\mathds{1}(V(G^{(0)}))
under
t
batches of updates
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)},...,\pi^{(t)}
of edge insertions/deletions, isolated vertex insertions/deletions and node-weighting insertions.
Given parameters
h
and
\phi
, there is an algorithm that maintains a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of some
\bar{A}
on
G
satisfying the following.

•

At any time
0\leq i\leq t
,

\bar{A}^{(i)}=A^{(i)}+\deg_{G^{(i)}}\geq\deg_{G^{(i)}}+\mathds{1}(V(G^{(i)}))
.

•

The integral moving cut
C
has initial size
|C^{(0)}|\leq O(|A^{(0)}|\cdot\phi\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{dynED},h}\cdot h)
, where

\lambda_{\mathrm{dynED},h}=2^{O(t)}=2^{O(1/\epsilon)}.

•

The landmark set
L
has initial size
|L^{(0)}|\leq O(|A^{(0)}|\cdot\phi)
. Over all time,
L
has distortion
\sigma=O(h\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{dynED},h}/\kappa_{\sigma})
, where
\kappa_{\sigma}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
is from
Theorem

4.4
.

•

{\cal N}
is a
b
-distributed
(h_{{\rm cov}},h_{\mathrm{sep}},\omega)
-neighborhood cover of
\bar{A}
on
G-C
with

b=O(t),\ h_{{\rm cov}}=h_{\mathrm{sep}}=h,\ \omega=t\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega},

where
\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
is from
Theorem

3.6
.

•

Each router in
{\cal R}
is maintained by
Theorem

5.1
under at most
f=O(t)
batched updates.

•

\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
is a
(h_{{\rm emb}},\gamma_{{\rm emb}})
-embedding with

\displaystyle h_{{\rm emb}}=O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{dynED},h}\cdot h),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\gamma_{{\rm emb}}=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{dynED},\gamma}/\phi),

where
\lambda_{{\rm init},h}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
will be defined in
Lemma

6.8
and

\kappa_{\mathrm{dynED},\gamma}=(\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(t)\cdot(t\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}+\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma})\cdot(\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}+\kappa_{{\rm init},L}))^{O(t)}=n^{O(\epsilon^{3})}.

Furthermore, at each update step
\pi^{(i)}
,

•

C^{(i)}=(C^{(i-1)})_{\mid E(G^{(i)})}+C^{(i)}_{{\rm new}}
with
|C^{(i)}_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(|\pi^{(i)}|\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{dynED},h}\cdot h)
.

•

L^{(i)}=(L^{(i-1)}\cap V(G^{(i)}))\cup L^{(i)}_{{\rm new}}
with
|L^{(i)}_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(|\pi^{(i)}|)
.

•

the recourse from
{\cal N}^{(i-1)}
and
{\cal N}^{(i)}
is

\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}^{(i-1)}\to{\cal N}^{(i)})=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(t)\cdot t\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{dynED},\gamma}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|/\phi)=n^{O(\epsilon^{3})}\cdot|\pi^{(i)}|/\phi.

The initialization time is
(|G^{(0)}|+|A^{(0)}|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
. The update time for a batch
\pi^{(i)}
is

|\pi^{(i)}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
.

We establish the proof of
Theorem

6.4
as follows. In
Section

6.1
, we show the initialize algorithm of certified-EDs. In
Sections

6.2
,
6.3
and
6.4
, we show the update algorithms for one batched update of pure node-weighting insertions, edge deletions and edge insertions respectively. Finally, in
Section

6.5
, we complete the proof of
Theorem

6.4
.

6.1
Initialization

Our initialization algorithm of certified-EDs is
Lemma

6.8
, which basically first computes a witnessed-ED by
3.18
, embeds our dynamic routers on it using expander routing
Theorem

3.19
, and initialzes the landmark set using
Lemma

6.5
.

6.5
Initialization of Landmarks.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A\geq\mathds{1}(V(G))
and an
(h,h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witnessed-ED
(C,{\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}}\to G-C})
. Let
\omega_{\mathrm{wit}}
be the width of
{\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}}
. Given a parameter
\sigma\leq h
, there is an algorithm that computes a new integral moving cut
C^{\prime}=C_{\mid\mathrm{supp}(C^{\prime})}
, i.e.
C^{\prime}
is the restriction of
C
on some edge set
\mathrm{supp}(C^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(C)
, and a landmark set
L
satisfying the following.

•

L
is a landmark set of
C^{\prime}
with distortion
\sigma
and size
|L|=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot|C^{\prime}|/\sigma)
.

•

For any simple path
P
on
G
with
\ell_{G-C}(P)\geq\sigma
, we have
\ell_{G-C}(P)/2\leq\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(P)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)
.

The running time is
(|G|+|A|)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

Proof.

We first construct a neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
of vertices
V(G)
(i.e. of node-weighting
\mathds{1}(V(G))
) on
G-C
with

h_{{\rm cov}}=\sigma/\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}},\ h_{{\rm diam}}=\sigma\text{ and }\omega=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}

by applying
Theorem

3.6
. Next, we construct
C^{\prime}
and
L
from
C
as follows. We will select some edges
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C)
(and set
C^{\prime}(e)=C(e)
) into the new cut
C^{\prime}
by the following two steps.

1.

For each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
, let
E_{C,S}
denote the edges in
\mathrm{supp}(C)
with
both
endpoints in
S
. If their total cut values
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
, we select
E_{C,S}
into
C^{\prime}
. Then we pick arbitrary
d(S)=\lceil|V(E_{C,S})|/(h_{{\rm cov}}/10)\rceil
many vertices in
S
as landmarks into
L
(we say these
d(S)
landmarks are
corresponding
to
S
). Note that we can choose such many vertices in
S
because
|S|\geq|V(E_{C,S})|\geq\lceil|V(E_{C,S})|/(h_{{\rm cov}}/10)\rceil
.

2.

Also, for each edge
e=(u,v)
with
\ell_{G-C}(e)\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
and
C(e)\geq\ell_{G-C}(e)/10
, we select
e
into
C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}(e)=C(e)
and add its endpoints
u
and
v
into
L
.

In other words, for each cut edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C)
not satisfying
\ell_{G-C}(e)\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
and
C(e)\geq\ell_{G-C}(e)/10
(i.e. the conditions in step
2
),
e
will be excluded from
C^{\prime}
if all clusters
S
covering
e
have small total cut values
C(E_{C,S})<h_{\rm cov}/10
.

Correctness.

The distortion and size of the
L
constructed above is shown by
6.6
. The second guarantee is shown by
6.7
.

6.6
.

L
is a landmark set of
C^{\prime}
on
G
with distortion
\sigma=h/\kappa_{\sigma}
. The size of
L
is bounded by
|L|\leq O(\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\sigma}\cdot|C^{\prime}|/h)
.

Proof.

For each edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C^{\prime})
with
\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(e)>\sigma
, note that
e
must be added in step
2
because
\ell_{G-C}(e)=\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(e)>\sigma\geq h_{{\rm diam}}
. Hence its endpoints will be added to
L
.

For each edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C^{\prime})
with
\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(e)\leq\sigma
, if it is added to
C^{\prime}
in step
2
, then trivially both its endpoints have themselves as landmarks. If
e
is added in step
1
via some cluster
S
, then the landmarks from this cluster will have
(G-C)
-distance at most
h_{{\rm diam}}\leq\sigma
to both
e
’s endpoints.

Next we bound the size of
L
. In step
1
, each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
will add
d(S)
landmarks if
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
. Because
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
and
|V(E_{C,S})|\leq 2|E_{C,S}|\leq 2\cdot C(E_{C,S})
, we have
d(S)\leq O(C(E_{C,S})/h_{{\rm cov}})
. Furthermore, because each edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C)
will appear in the set
E_{C,S}
of at most
\omega
clusters
S
(one from each clustering), we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in{\cal N}\text{
s.t. }\\
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}C(E_{C,S})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in{\cal N}\text{
s.t. }\\
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}C^{\prime}(E_{C,S})\leq\omega\cdot|C^{\prime}|,

where the first equality is because we add
E_{C,S}
to
C^{\prime}
with the same cut values for all
S
s.t.
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}/10}
.
Therefore, the total number of landmarks added in step
1
can be bounded by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in{\cal N}\text{
s.t. }\\
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}d(S)\leq O(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in{\cal N}\text{
s.t. }\\
C(E_{C,S})\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}C(E_{C,S})/h_{{\rm cov}})\leq O(\omega\cdot|C^{\prime}|/h_{{\rm cov}}).

For step
2
, each edge that puts its endpoints into
L
have
C^{\prime}(e)=C(e)\geq\ell_{G-C}(e)/10\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/100
. Hence the number of landmarks added in step
2
is bounded by
O(|C^{\prime}|/h_{{\rm cov}})
. In conclusion

|L|\leq O(\omega\cdot|C^{\prime}|/h_{{\rm cov}})+O(|C^{\prime}|/h_{{\rm cov}})=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot|C^{\prime}|/\sigma).

∎

6.7
.

For an arbitrary simple path
P
with
\ell_{G-C}(P)\geq h_{{\rm cov}}
, we have
\ell_{G-C}(P)/2\leq\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(P)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)
.

Proof.

First,
\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(P)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)
holds trivially because
C^{\prime}
is a subset of
C
. To show
\ell_{G-C}(P)/2\leq\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(P)
, consider the moving cut we drop on
P
, denoted by
C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}=(C-C^{\prime})_{\mid P}
, and we will show it has size at most a constant fraction of
\ell_{G-C}(P)
, i.e.
C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}(P)\leq 1/2\cdot\ell_{G-C}(P)
.

For each
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})
, if
\ell_{G-C}(e)\geq h_{{\rm cov}}/10
, it must have
C(e)<\ell_{G-C}(e)/10
, Otherwise it has been included in
C^{\prime}
in step
Item

2
. Therefore,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}),\\
\text{ s.t. }\ell_{G-C}(e)\geq h_{{\rm cov}/10}\end{subarray}}C(e)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)/10.

For those edges
e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})
s.t.
\ell_{G-C}(e)<h_{{\rm cov}}/10
, we use a charging argument. For each such edge
e
, we charge
g(e^{\prime},e)=C(e)/h_{{\rm cov}}
dollar to each edge
e^{\prime}\in P
s.t.
one
endpoint of
e^{\prime}
has
P
-distance at most
h_{{\rm cov}}
to
both
endpoints of
e
, where the
P
-distance between two vertices on
P
is the

(G-C)
-length of subpath between them. Now, let
g(e^{\prime})
be the total dollars charging to
e^{\prime}
in the whole charging process. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}),\\
\text{s.t. }\ell_{G-C}(e)<h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}C(e)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}),\\
\text{s.t. }\ell_{G-C}(e)<h_{{\rm cov}}/10\end{subarray}}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in P\text{ charged by $e$}}(C(e)/h_{{\rm cov}}))\cdot\ell_{G-C}(e^{\prime})\leq\sum_{e^{\prime}\in P}\ell_{G-C}(e^{\prime})\cdot g(e^{\prime})

where the first inequality holds because the edges
e^{\prime}
charged by
e
have total
(G-C)
-length at least
h_{{\rm cov}}
, which is true because
\ell_{G-C}(P)\geq h_{{\rm cov}}
and
\ell_{G-C}(e)<h_{{\rm cov}}/10\leq h_{\rm cov}
16
16

16

For better understanding, here we need
\ell_{G-C}(e)\leq h_{{\rm cov}}
because there would have been no edge
e^{\prime}
charged by
e
if
\ell_{G-C}(e)>h_{{\rm cov}}
.
.

Then, all we need is an upper bound on
g(e^{\prime})
for each
e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in P
. By our charging process, each edge
e=(u,v)
that charges
e^{\prime}
must have both
u
and
v
with
(G-C)
-distance to
u^{\prime}
(or
v^{\prime}
) at most
h_{{\rm cov}}
. Let
S_{u^{\prime}}
and
S_{v^{\prime}}
be the clusters in
{\cal N}
covering
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},h_{{\rm cov}})
and
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C}(v^{\prime},h_{{\rm cov}})
respectively. Then either
e\in E_{C,S_{u^{\prime}}}
or
e\in E_{C,S_{v^{\prime}}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle g(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in E_{C,S_{u^{\prime}}}\cap\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})}g(e^{\prime},e)+\sum_{e\in E_{C,S_{v^{\prime}}}\cap\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})}g(e^{\prime},e)

\displaystyle=(\sum_{e\in E_{C,S_{u^{\prime}}\cap\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})}}C(e)+\sum_{e\in E_{C,S_{v^{\prime}}}\cap\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})}C(e))/h_{{\rm cov}}

\displaystyle\leq(2h_{{\rm cov}}/10)/h_{{\rm cov}}=1/5.

The last inequality is because each cluster
S
in
{\cal N}
will either keep all moving cuts in
C^{\prime}
or drop all of them if
C(E_{C,S})<h_{{\rm cov}}/10
.

Finally, we can conclude

\sum_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(C_{P,\mathrm{drop}})}C(e)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)\cdot(1/10+1/5)\leq\ell_{G-C}(P)/2,

so
\ell_{G-C^{\prime}}(P)=\ell_{G-C}(P)-C_{P,\mathrm{drop}}(P)\geq\ell_{G-C}(P)/2
.

∎

The Running Time.

The construction of
{\cal N}
takes
(|G|+|A|)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time by
Theorem

3.6
. The construction of
C^{\prime}
and
L
just needs to scan
{\cal N}
and
|E(G)|
, which takes
\widetilde{O}(\mathrm{size}({\cal N})+|E(G)|)\leq\widetilde{O}(\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot|G|)=|G|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time.

∎

6.8
Initialization of Certified ED.

Given a graph
G
with a node-weighting
A\geq\mathds{1}(V(G))
and parameters
h
and
\phi
, there is an algorithm that computes a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
satisfying the following.

•

|C|\leq O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi\cdot h\cdot|A|)
where
\kappa_{{\rm init},C}=\kappa_{\mathrm{ED},C}\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
.

•

L
has distortion
\sigma=h/\kappa_{\sigma}
and size
|L|=O(\kappa_{{\rm init},L}\cdot\phi\cdot|A|)
, where

\displaystyle\kappa_{{\rm init},L}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\kappa_{\sigma}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}.

•

{\cal N}
is a
b
-distributed
(h_{{\rm cov}},h_{\mathrm{sep}},h_{{\rm diam}},\omega)
-neighborhood cover of
A
on
G-C
with

b=1,\ h_{{\rm cov}}=h,\ h_{\mathrm{sep}}=h,\text{ and }\omega=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega},

where
\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
is from
Theorem

3.6
.
Furthermore, if a cluster
S
includes a virtual node of some vertex
v
, then
S
includes all virtual nodes of
v
.

•

Routers
{\cal R}
are initialized by
Theorem

5.1
(undergoing
f=0
batched update).

•

Embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
has length
h_{{\rm emb}}=O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h)
and congestion
\gamma_{{\rm emb}}=O(\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma}/\phi)
where

\displaystyle\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{{\rm emb},\gamma}=n^{O(\epsilon^{4})},

\displaystyle\lambda_{{\rm init},h}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{{\rm emb},h}\cdot\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{ED},h}\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}.

The running time is
(|G|+|A|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

Proof.

By applying
3.18
on
G
and
A
with parameters
h_{\mathrm{wit}}=2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}}=2\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot h
and
\phi
, we can compute an
(h_{\mathrm{wit}},h_{\cal R},\gamma_{\cal R},h_{\Pi},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witnessed-ED
(C_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}}\to G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}})
satisfying the following.

•

|C_{\mathrm{wit}}|\leq\kappa_{\mathrm{ED},C}\cdot h_{\mathrm{wit}}\cdot\phi\cdot|A|
.

•

{\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}}
is a neighborhood cover of
V(G)
on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
with cover radius
h_{\mathrm{wit}}
and width
\omega_{\mathrm{wit}}=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
.

•

h_{\cal R}=\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}
,
h_{\Pi}=\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}\cdot h_{\mathrm{wit}}
,
\gamma_{\cal R}=1
and
\gamma_{\Pi}=1/\phi
.

Next, we apply
Lemma

6.5
on
G
and the witnessed-ED
(C_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}}\to G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}})
. The output is a new integral moving cut
C=(C_{\mathrm{wit}})_{\mid\mathrm{supp}(C)}
and a landmark set
L
of
C
on
G
with distortion
\sigma=h/\kappa_{\sigma}
and size

|L|=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot|C|/\sigma)\leq O(\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\sigma}\cdot|C_{\mathrm{wit}}|/h)=O(\kappa_{{\rm init},L}\cdot\phi\cdot|A|)

The neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
can be initialized by invoking
Theorem

3.6
on graph
G-C
with node-weighting
A
and length parameter
h
, so it will have
h_{{\rm cov}}=h
,
h_{\mathrm{sep}}=h
,
\omega=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
and diameter
h_{{\rm diam}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot h
. Next, we use
Theorem

5.1
to initialize an router
R^{S}
with
V(R^{S})=S
for each cluster in
S\in{\cal N}
. In particular, initially there is no redundant vertex in the router
R^{S}
. Let
{\cal R}=\{R^{S}\mid S\in{\cal N}\}
collect all the routers.

Lastly, we need to compute the embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
. To clarify, recall that each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
is a subset of virtual nodes in
A
, so each router vertex
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in V(R^{S})
will corresponding to a virtual node
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in A
, and when defining the embedding, we will map
v
to the vertex
v_{\mathrm{vtx}}\in V(G)
that owns
v
. Then we define a demand
D
on vertices
V(G)
by adding one unit of demand to
D(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})
for each router edge
e=(u_{\mathrm{vtul}},v_{\mathrm{vtul}})\in E({\cal R})
. We apply
Theorem

3.19
on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
and its witness
({\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}}\to G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}})
with
D
as the input demand. Note that the input satisfies the requirements of
Theorem

3.19
, because by definition
({\cal N}_{\mathrm{wit}},{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{\mathrm{wit}}\to G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}})
is also a
(h_{\mathrm{wit}},h_{\cal R},\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\gamma_{\cal R},\gamma_{\Pi})
-witness of
(\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot A
on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
, and by
6.9
,
D
is an
h_{\mathrm{wit}}
-length
((\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot A)
-respecting demand on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
.
The output is an integral routing of
D
on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
with

\displaystyle h_{{\rm emb}}

\displaystyle=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot h_{\cal R}\cdot h_{\Pi})=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{emb},h}\cdot\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{ED},h}\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot h),

\displaystyle\gamma_{{\rm emb}}

\displaystyle=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{emb},\gamma}\cdot\gamma_{\cal R}\cdot\gamma_{\Pi})=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{emb},\gamma}/\phi).

We can simply assign flow paths of the integral routing of
D
to each unit of demand, and then obtain the embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
with the same bounds on length and congestion.

6.9
.

D
is a
((\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot A)
-respecting demand on
G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}
with length
h_{\mathrm{wit}}=2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}}
.

Proof.

For each virtual node
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in A
, it will appear in at most
\omega=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
many clusters of
{\cal N}
. For each cluster
S\ni v_{\mathrm{vtul}}
, the router vertex in
R^{S}
corresponding to
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}
has degree at most
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}
. Hence there are totally
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
many router edges connecting a virtual node
v
summing over all routers. Then for each vertex
v_{\mathrm{vtx}}\in V(G)
, there are at most
\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot A(v)
many router edges with endpoint mapped to
v_{\mathrm{vtx}}
, so
D
is
((\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot A)
-respecting.

Now we show that for each demand pair
(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})\in\mathrm{supp}(D)
,
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})\leq 2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}}
. Consider a router edge
e=(u_{\mathrm{vtul}},v_{\mathrm{vtul}})
which adds one unit of demand to
D(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})
. Then
u_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in A(u_{\mathrm{vtx}})
and
v_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in A(v_{\mathrm{vtx}})
are virtual nodes owned by
u_{\mathrm{vtx}}
and
v_{\mathrm{vtx}}
respectively. Because
e
is a router edge, there is a cluster
S\in{\cal N}
s.t.
u_{\mathrm{vtul}},v_{\mathrm{vtul}}\in S
, which implies
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})\leq h_{{\rm diam}}
. Recall that
Lemma

6.5
guarantees, for any simple path
P
on
G
with
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(P)\geq\sigma
, we have
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(P)\leq 2\cdot\ell_{G-C}(P)
. Consider the shortest
u_{\mathrm{vtx}}
-to-
v_{\mathrm{vtx}}
path
P
on
G-C
. If
\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(P)\geq\sigma
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})\leq\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(P)\leq 2\cdot\ell_{G-C}(P)\leq 2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}}
, otherwise trivially
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(u_{\mathrm{vtx}},v_{\mathrm{vtx}})\leq\ell_{G-C_{\mathrm{wit}}}(P)<\sigma\leq 2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}}
.
∎

The Running Time.

Computing the witnessed-ED takes
|G|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(2\cdot h_{{\rm diam}})\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time by
3.18
.
Computing
C
and its landmark set
L
also takes
|G|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}

time by
Lemma

6.5
.
Constructing the neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
takes
(|G|+|A|)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time by
Theorem

3.6
. Initializing the routers takes
(|A|\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time by
Theorem

5.1
, because the total size of clusters is
\mathrm{size}({\cal N})\leq O(|A|\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})
. Lastly, computing the embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
takes
(|A|\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}+|A|\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot\omega_{\mathrm{wit}})\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h_{{\rm diam}})\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{ED},h}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
time by
Theorem

3.19
, because this step we works with node-weighting
(\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},\deg}\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot A
. In conclusion, the total time is
(|G|+|A|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

∎

6.2
Batched Node-Weighting Insertion

To insert a node-weighting
A_{{\rm new}}
into a certified-ED of an old node-weighting
A
, our strategy is to compute a cutmatch between
A_{{\rm new}}
and
A
using our local cutmatch algorithm by
Theorem

4.4
.
A_{{\rm new}}
is partitioned into a matching part
A_{{\rm new},M}
and an unmatched part
A_{{\rm new},U}
. Intuitively, we can just add the matching part
A_{{\rm new},M}
into the old certified-ED because the matching has low length and congestion. For the unmatched part, we can initialize a new certified-ED of
A_{{\rm new},U}
on a
local
subgraph, because
A_{{\rm new},U}
is far from most of the old virtual nodes by adding a small new cut.

6.10
.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A
and a certified-ED
(C,{L},{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
in
Definition

6.1
.
Given a node-weighting
A_{{\rm new}}
s.t.
\widetilde{A}=A+A_{{\rm new}}\geq\deg_{G}+\mathds{1}(V(G))
and parameters
h
and
\phi
, there is an algorithm that computes a certified-ED
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})
of
\widetilde{A}
on
G
s.t.

•

\widetilde{C}=C+C_{{\rm new}}
with
|C_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi\cdot h\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|)
.

•

\widetilde{L}=L\cup{L}_{{\rm new}}
with distortion
\widetilde{\sigma}=\sigma+O(h/\kappa_{\sigma})
and
|{L}_{{\rm new}}|=O((\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}+\kappa_{{\rm init},L})\cdot\phi\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|)
.

•

\widetilde{\cal N}
is a
\widetilde{b}
-distributed
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}},\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}},\widetilde{\omega})
-neighborhood cover of
\widetilde{A}
on
G-\widetilde{C}
with

\widetilde{b}=b+1,\ \widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h/3,\ \widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}=h/3,\text{ and }\widetilde{\omega}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}.

•

Routers in
\widetilde{\cal R}
are maintained by
Theorem

5.1
under
\widetilde{f}=f+1
update batches.

•

\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G}
is a
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}},\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}})
-embedding with

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}}=\max\{h_{{\rm emb}},O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h)\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}}=\gamma_{{\rm emb}}+O((\omega\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}+\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma})/\phi).

The recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
is
\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})=O(|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}))
.
The running time is
|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega)
.

Proof.

We first apply the cutmatch subroutine,
Theorem

4.4
, on
G-C
with source node-weighting
A^{\mathrm{src}}=A_{{\rm new}}
, sink node-weighting
A_{\mathrm{sink}}=A
and parameters
h_{\mathrm{CM}}=h/3
and
\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}=\phi
. The output is

•

a partition
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
of
A^{\mathrm{src}}
,

•

a partition
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M},A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}
of
A^{\mathrm{sink}}
with
|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|=O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|)
,

•

a matching
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
between
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}
with size
|M_{\mathrm{CM}}|=|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|
and its
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-length embedding
\Pi_{M_{\mathrm{CM}}\to G-C}
which has congestion
\gamma_{\mathrm{CM}}=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}})
, and

•

a moving cut
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
with size
|C_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|
s.t.
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
is
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separated from
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}
in
G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}
.

•

a landmark set
L_{\mathrm{CM}}
of
C_{\mathrm{CM}}
on
G-C
with distortion
\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}=O(h/\kappa_{\sigma})
and size
|L_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq O(\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|)

We define two node-weightings
A_{1}=A^{\mathrm{sink}}\cup A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}=A\cup A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
and

A_{2}=\widetilde{A}\setminus\{\text{virtual nodes of vertices in }\mathrm{supp}(A_{U}^{\mathrm{sink}})\}.

For better understanding, equivalently
A_{2}=(A^{\mathrm{src}}\cup A_{M}^{\mathrm{sink}})\setminus\{\text{virtual nodes of vertices in }\mathrm{supp}(A_{U}^{\mathrm{sink}})\}
.
Note that
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}\subseteq A_{2}
because
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
is
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separated from
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
(it means
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
are disjoint).
A_{2}
has size
|A_{2}|\leq|A_{{\rm new}}|+|A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}|\leq|A_{{\rm new}}|+O(|A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}|)\leq O(|A_{{\rm new}}|)
.

We first compute
({\cal N}_{1},{\cal R}_{1},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{1}\to G})
by updating
({\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
. Concretely, for each original cluster
S\in{\cal N}
and its router
R\in{\cal R}
, let
M_{S}=\{(u,v)\in M_{\mathrm{CM}}\mid\text{the sink virtual node $v\in S$}\}

collect all matching edges in
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
with one endpoint falling in
S
. Then we update
S
and
R
to
S_{1}=S\cup M^{\mathrm{src}}_{S}
(recall that
M^{\mathrm{src}}_{S}
denotes the source virtual nodes in
M_{S}
) and
R_{1}=R\cup M_{S}
via a matching insertion update in
Theorem

5.1
. Moreover, the new router
R_{1}
has embedding
\Pi_{R_{1}\to G}=\Pi_{R\to G}\cup\Pi_{M_{S}\to G}
, where
\Pi_{M_{S}\to G}
can be obtained from
\Pi_{M_{\mathrm{CM}}\to G-C}
(note that the graphs
G
and
G-C
have the same structure).

Next, we initialize a certified-ED on
(G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}})[A_{2}]
by applying
Lemma

6.8
with node-weighting
A_{2}
and parameters
\phi_{\mathrm{ED}}=\phi
and
h_{\mathrm{ED}}=h/3
. The output
(C_{\mathrm{ED}},L_{\mathrm{ED}},{\cal N}_{2},{\cal R}_{2},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{2}\to G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}})
satisfies the following.

•

|C_{\mathrm{ED}}|\leq O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot|A_{2}|)
.

•

L_{\mathrm{ED}}
is a landmark set of
C_{\mathrm{ED}}
on
(G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}})[A_{2}]
with distortion
\sigma_{\mathrm{ED}}=O(h_{\mathrm{ED}}/\kappa_{\sigma})
and size
|L_{\mathrm{ED}}|=O(\kappa_{{\rm init},L}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot|A_{2}|)
. Note that
L_{\mathrm{ED}}
is automatically a landmark set of
C_{\mathrm{ED}}
on
G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}
with the same distortion and size.

•

{\cal N}_{2}
is an
(h_{{\rm cov},2},h_{\mathrm{sep},2},\omega_{2})
-neighborhood cover of
A_{2}
on
G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}-C_{\mathrm{ED}}
with
h_{{\rm cov},2}=h_{\mathrm{ED}},h_{\mathrm{sep},2}=h_{\mathrm{ED}},h_{{\rm diam}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{NC},{\rm diam}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{ED}}
and
\omega_{2}=\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
.

•

Routers in
{\cal R}_{2}
are initialized by
Theorem

5.1
.

•

The embedding
\Pi_{{\cal R}_{2}\to(G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}})[A_{2}]}
has congestion
\gamma_{{\rm emb},2}=O(\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma}/\phi_{\mathrm{ED}})
and dilation
h_{{\rm emb},2}=O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h_{\mathrm{ED}})
.

The new certified-ED
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\cal N},\widetilde{\cal R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}=C+C_{{\rm new}}\text{ where }C_{{\rm new}}=C_{\mathrm{CM}}+C_{\mathrm{ED}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}=L\cup{L}_{{\rm new}}\text{ where }{L}_{{\rm new}}=L_{\mathrm{CM}}\cup L_{\mathrm{ED}},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\cal N},\widetilde{\cal R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})=({\cal N}_{1},{\cal R}_{1},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{1}\to G})\cup({\cal N}_{2},{\cal R}_{2},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{2}\to G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}}).

The Quality.

The quality of
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\cal N},\widetilde{\cal R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})
can be verified as follows.
The new moving cut has size

|C_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|)+O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot|A_{2}|)\leq O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi\cdot h\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|)

because
h_{\mathrm{ED}}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}=h/3
,
|A^{\mathrm{src}}|=|A_{{\rm new}}|
and
|A_{2}|\leq O(|A_{{\rm new}}|)
. By
Lemma

3.21
,
\widetilde{L}=L\cup L_{\mathrm{CM}}\cup L_{\mathrm{ED}}
is a landmark set of
\widetilde{C}=C+C_{\mathrm{CM}}+C_{\mathrm{ED}}
on
G
with distortion
\widetilde{\sigma}=\sigma+\sigma_{\mathrm{CM}}+\sigma_{\mathrm{ED}}=\sigma+O(h/\kappa_{\sigma})
, size

|L_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|)+O(\kappa_{{\rm init},L}\cdot\phi_{\mathrm{ED}}\cdot|A_{2}|)\leq O((\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}+\kappa_{{\rm init},L})\cdot\phi\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|).

The quality of
\widetilde{\cal N}
is shown in
Lemma

6.11
. In particular, the ball cover
{\cal S}_{v}
of each
v\in\widetilde{A}
can be maintained explicitly following the proof of
Lemma

6.11
. The routers from
{\cal R}_{1}
are updated from
{\cal R}
via one batched update, and
{\cal R}_{2}
are just initialized with no update. Lastly, the length and congestion of
\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G}
are

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}}

\displaystyle=\max\{h_{{\rm emb}},h_{\mathrm{CM}},h_{{\rm emb},2}\}=\max\{h_{{\rm emb}},O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h)\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{{\rm emb}}+\omega\cdot\gamma_{\mathrm{CM}}+\gamma_{{\rm emb},2}=\gamma_{{\rm emb}}+O((\omega\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}+\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma})/\phi).

The term
\gamma_{\mathrm{CM}}
is multiplied by
\omega
because each matching edge will be added to routers at most
\omega
times.

6.11
.

\widetilde{\cal N}
is a
\widetilde{b}
-distributed
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}},\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}},\widetilde{\omega})
-pairwise cover of
\widetilde{A}
on
G-\widetilde{C}
with
\widetilde{b}=b+1
,
\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h/3
,
\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}=h/3
and
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
.

Proof.

It is relatively simple to see the bounds on
\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\widetilde{\omega}
. The width
\widetilde{\omega}=\omega+\omega_{2}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
. Regarding the separation, note that
\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}=\max\{h_{\mathrm{sep},1},h_{\mathrm{sep},2}\}
, where
h_{\mathrm{sep},1}
denotes the separation of
{\cal N}_{1}
, and
h_{\mathrm{sep},2}
is already known to be
h_{\mathrm{ED}}=h/3
. To see
h_{\mathrm{sep},1}=h/3
, we need to show that for two virtual nodes
u,v\in A_{1}
belonging to two different clusters
S_{u},S_{v}\in{\cal N}_{1}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)>h/3
. Consider the worst case that
u,v\in A_{1}\cap A_{{\rm new}}
are both new virtual nodes. Let
u^{\prime}\in S_{u}
and
v^{\prime}\in S_{v}
be the old virtual nodes matched to
u
and
v
in
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
respectively. The separation of
{\cal N}
gives
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\geq h_{\mathrm{sep}}=h
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})-\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,u^{\prime})-\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle>h_{\mathrm{sep}}-2h_{\mathrm{CM}}=h/3.

The case that at most one of
u,v
belongs to
A_{1}\cap A_{{\rm new}}
can be argued in a similar way.

We now show that
\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h/3
and
\widetilde{b}=b+1
. Consider an arbitrary virtual node
v\in\widetilde{A}
.

Case 1.
Suppose
v\in A\setminus A_{2}
. That is,
v
is a virtual node of some vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
. By definition of
{\cal N}
, we know
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C,A}(v,h_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by a collection
{\cal S}_{v}=\{S_{1},...,S_{b}\}\subseteq{\cal N}
of
b
clusters. We will show that
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by
b
many clusters
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v}=\{\widetilde{S}_{1},...,\widetilde{S}_{b}\}\subseteq{\cal N}_{1}
, where each new cluster
\widetilde{S}_{b^{\prime}}
originates from the old cluster
S_{b^{\prime}}
. Let
u\in\widetilde{A}
be an arbitrary virtual node s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}
.

Subcase 1(a).
If
u\in A
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}\leq h_{{\rm cov}}
, so
u
is inside some old cluster
S\in{\cal S}_{v}
and the new cluster
\widetilde{S}\in\widetilde{\cal S}_{v}
.

Subcase 1(b).
Suppose
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
. Let
u^{\prime}\in A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}\subseteq A
be the virtual node matched to
u
by
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},v)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},u)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},u)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq h_{\mathrm{CM}}+\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}\leq h_{{\rm cov}},

which means
u^{\prime}
belongs to some old cluster
S\in{\cal S}_{v}
. The new cluster
\widetilde{S}\in\widetilde{S}_{v}
will include
u
by our construction.

Subcase 1(c).
Suppose
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
. However, this is impossible because
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}
contradicts that
A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
is
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separated from
A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U}
in
G-\widetilde{C}
.

Case 2.
Suppose
v\in A\cap A_{2}
. Again,
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C}(v,h_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by a size-
b
collection
{\cal S}_{v}\subseteq{\cal N}
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{Ball}_{(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}]}(v,h_{\mathrm{ED}})
is covered by a cluster
\widetilde{S}_{v,2}\in{\cal N}_{2}
because
{\cal N}_{2}
, is a (1-distributed) neighborhood cover. We now show that
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by
b+1
many clusters in
\widetilde{\cal N}
, i.e. the
\widetilde{S}_{v,2}\in{\cal N}_{2}
and the new clusters
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v,1}\subseteq{\cal N}_{1}
original from
{\cal S}_{v}
. Consider an arbitrary
u\in\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
.

Subcases 2(a) and 2(b).
If
u\in A
or
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
, then
u
belongs to some cluster in
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v}
by the same arguments as subcases 1(a) and 1(b) respectively.

Subcase 2(c).
Suppose
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}\subseteq A_{2}
. Then
\mathrm{dist}_{(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}]}(u,v)=\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h_{\mathrm{ED}}
by
6.12
, so
u
belongs to
\widetilde{S}_{v}
.

6.12
.

For
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
and
v\in A_{2}
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}]}(u,v)=\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)
.

Proof.

Consider the shortest
u
-
v
path
P
on
G-\widetilde{C}
. We know
\ell_{G-\widetilde{C}}(P)=\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h_{\mathrm{CM}}
, so
P
cannot go through any vertex
w\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{A}\setminus A_{2})=\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
because
w
is
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separated from
u
even on
G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}
(let alone
G-\widetilde{C}
). Therefore,
P
is totally inside
(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}]
and the observation holds.
∎

Case 3.
Suppose
v\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}\setminus A_{2}
. This infers
v
is owned by some vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
. Let
v^{\prime}\in A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}\subseteq A
be the virtual node matched to
v
by
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
. Let
{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}\subseteq{\cal N}
be those
b
clusters covering
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C,A}(v^{\prime},h_{{\rm cov}})
and let
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}\subseteq{\cal N}_{1}
be the clusters original from
{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}
. Observe that, for every
S\in\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
, we have
v\in S
because
v
is matched to
v^{\prime}
by
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
and so our algorithm adds
v
to every cluster
S\in\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
. We will next show that
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by the new clusters
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
. Consider an arbitrary virtual node
u\in\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
.

Subcase 3(a).
If
u\in A
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}+h_{\mathrm{CM}}\leq h_{{\rm cov}},

and
{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}
covers
u
, so does
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
.

Subcase 3(b).
If
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
, let
u^{\prime}\in A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}\subseteq A
be the virtual node matched to
u
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},u)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u^{\prime},u)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-\widetilde{C}}(u,v)+\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq h_{\mathrm{CM}}+\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}+h_{\mathrm{CM}}\leq h_{{\rm cov}},

so
u^{\prime}
is covered by some cluster
S\in{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}
. By the construction,
u
will be added to
S
, so
u
is covered by
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
.

Subcase 3(c).
It is impossible that
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
because
v
is owned by some vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
and
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U})
is
(h_{\mathrm{CM}}=\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
-separated from
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
in
G-\widetilde{C}
.

Case 4.
Suppose
v\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}\cap A_{2}
which matches
v^{\prime}\in A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{M}\subseteq A
in
M_{\mathrm{CM}}
. Again, let
{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}\subseteq{\cal N}
be the
b
clusters covering
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-C,A}(v^{\prime},h_{{\rm cov}})
. Moreover, let
\widetilde{S}_{v,2}\in{\cal N}_{2}
be the cluster covering
\mathrm{Ball}_{(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}],A_{2}}(v,h_{\mathrm{ED}})
. Then we will show that
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
is covered by
\widetilde{S}_{v}
and the new clusters
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}\subseteq{\cal N}_{1}
original from
{\cal S}_{v^{\prime},1}
.

Subcases 4(a) and 4(b).
If
u\in A
and
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{M}
,
\widetilde{\cal S}_{v^{\prime}}
covers
u
by the same argument as subcases 3(a) and 3(b) respectively.

Subcase 4(c).
If
u\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}\subseteq A_{2}
, then
u\in\widetilde{S}_{v}
by the same argument as subcase 2(c).

Case 5.
Suppose
v\in A^{\mathrm{src}}_{U}
. Consider an arbitrary
u\in\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
. We know
u
is not owned by any vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(A^{\mathrm{sink}}_{U})
by the
h_{\mathrm{CM}}
-separation, so
u\in A_{2}
. By
6.12
, the cluster
\widetilde{S}_{v}\in{\cal N}_{2}
covering
\mathrm{Ball}_{(G-\widetilde{C})[A_{2}],A_{2}}(v,h_{\mathrm{ED}})
will cover
\mathrm{Ball}_{G-\widetilde{C},\widetilde{A}}(v,\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}})
.

∎

The Recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
.

First,
{\cal N}_{1}
can be updated from
{\cal N}
by at most
|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\omega
virtual node insertions, because each virtual node in
A_{{\rm new}}
can be inserted at most
\omega
times (at most once for each clustering). Second,
\widetilde{\cal N}
can be updated from
{\cal N}_{1}
by adding
{\cal N}_{2}
, where
{\cal N}_{2}
has at most
\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}\cdot|A_{2}|
virtual nodes summing over all clusters. Therefore, the recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
is

\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})\leq|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\omega+|A_{2}|\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}=O(|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})).

The Running Time.

The running time of the cutmatch subroutine is
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{CM}})\cdot|A^{\mathrm{src}}|/\phi_{\mathrm{CM}}
by
Theorem

4.4
. When we update
({\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
to
({\cal N}_{1},{\cal R}_{1},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{1}\to G})
, the running time is dominated by updating the routers.
The total size of matchings added to routers is
\sum_{S\in{\cal N}}|M_{S}|\leq\omega\cdot|M_{\mathrm{CM}}|\leq\omega\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|
because each matching edge appears at most
\omega
times. By
Theorem

5.1
, the total time to update routers is
2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega\cdot|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
. By
Lemma

6.8
, the construction time of
(C_{\mathrm{ED}},L_{\mathrm{ED}},{\cal N}_{2},{\cal R}_{2},\Pi_{{\cal R}_{2}\to G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}}})
is
|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{ED}})\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
, because the size of subgraph
(G-C-C_{\mathrm{CM}})[A_{2}]
is bounded by
\deg_{G}(\mathrm{supp}(A_{2}))\leq|A_{2}|\leq O(|A_{{\rm new}}|)
, where
\deg_{G}(\mathrm{supp}(A_{2}))\leq|A_{2}|
follows that each vertex
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A_{2})
has
A_{2}(v)\geq\deg_{G}(v)
. In conclusion, the total running time is bounded by
|A_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega)
.

∎

6.3
Batched Edge Deletions

A batched edge deletion update to the certified-ED can be reduced to a batched node-weighting insertion. The key point is that we have a low-congestion embedding of routers on
G
, which means deleting one edge will only destroy a small number of router edges. Removing these router edges from our dynamic routers will only generate a small set of pruned virtual nodes. Then we can reinsert the pruned virtual node by a batched node-weighint insertion.

6.13
.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A\geq\deg_{G}+\mathds{1}(V(G))
and a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
in
Definition

6.1
.
Given parameters
\phi,h
, and a batch of edge deletions
F\subseteq E(G)
, there is an algorithm that computes a certified-ED
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})
of
\widetilde{A}=A-\deg_{F}
on
\widetilde{G}=G\setminus F
s.t.

•

\widetilde{C}=C+C_{{\rm new}}
with

|C_{{\rm new}}|\leq O(\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi\cdot h\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|)
.

•

\widetilde{L}=L\cup L_{{\rm new}}
with distortion
\widetilde{\sigma}=\sigma+O(h/\kappa_{\sigma})
and

|L_{{\rm new}}|=O((\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}+\kappa_{{\rm init},L})\cdot\phi\cdot\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|).

•

\widetilde{\cal N}
is a
\widetilde{b}
-distributed
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}},\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}},\widetilde{\omega})
-neighborhood cover of
A
on
G-\widetilde{C}
with

\widetilde{b}=b+1,\ \widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h/3,\ \widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}=h/3,\text{ and }\widetilde{\omega}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}.

•

Routers in
\widetilde{\cal R}
is maintained by
Theorem

5.1
undergoing at most
\widetilde{f}=f+2
batched updates.

•

\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to\widetilde{G}}
is a
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}},\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}})
-embedding with

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}}=\max\{h_{{\rm emb}},O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h)\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}}=\gamma_{{\rm emb}}+O((\omega\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}+\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma})/\phi).

The recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
is

\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})=O(\kappa_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}(f)}\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|F|).

The running time is

\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega)
.

Proof.

Let
{\cal R}_{\mathrm{edge},F}=\Pi^{-1}_{{\cal R}\to G}(F)
collect all edges in routers with embedding paths destroyed by
F
.
Then for each cluster
S\in{\cal N}
and its router
R\in{\cal R}
, let
R_{\mathrm{edge},F}={\cal R}_{\mathrm{edge},F}\cap E(R)
and we perform an edge deletion update
R_{\mathrm{edge},F}
on
R
by applying
Theorem

5.1
, which outputs a prune set
R_{{\rm prune}}\subseteq V(R)
and a new router
R^{\prime}=R[V(R)\setminus R_{{\rm prune}}]
. Let
S_{{\rm prune}}=R_{{\rm prune}}\cap S
be the set of pruned virtual nodes of
S
(namely, we obtain
S_{{\rm prune}}
by excluding redundant vertices in
R_{{\rm prune}}
). Let
A_{{\rm prune}}=\bigcup_{S\in{\cal N}}S_{{\rm prune}}
collect all pruned virtual nodes.

Now we partition
A
into
A_{{\rm prune}}
and
A^{\prime}=A\setminus A_{{\rm prune}}
. Then we update
(C,L,{\cal N},R,\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
to a certified-ED
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
of
A^{\prime}
on
\widetilde{G}
by removing
A_{{\rm prune}}
. Concretely, for each original cluster
S\in{\cal S}
and its router
R\in{\cal R}
, we will update them to a new cluster
S^{\prime}
corresponding the new router
R^{\prime}=R[V(R)\setminus R_{{\rm prune}}]
by the following steps.

1.

Remove
A_{{\rm prune}}\cap S
from
S
and denote this new cluster by
S^{\prime}
. Note that the new router
R^{\prime}=R[V(R)\setminus R_{{\rm prune}}]
satisfies
S^{\prime}=S\setminus A_{{\rm prune}}\subseteq V(R)\setminus R_{{\rm prune}}=V(R^{\prime})
, so
R^{\prime}
is a valid router for
S^{\prime}
. Technically, some vertices in
V(R^{\prime})
may still correspond to some virtual nodes in
A_{{\rm prune}}
(precisely, these virtual nodes are
A_{{\rm prune}}\setminus S_{{\rm prune}}
), so we will mark all such vertices in
R^{\prime}
redundant.

2.

For each edge removed from
R
due to the previous update via
Theorem

5.1
, remove its embedding path from
\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G}
.

Lastly, we let
C^{\prime}=C_{\mid E(\widetilde{G})}
by removing cut values on deleted edges (this step is just to make
C^{\prime}
well-defined on
E(\widetilde{G})
). Also, we let
L^{\prime}=L\cup L^{\prime}_{{\rm new}}
with
L^{\prime}_{{\rm new}}=V(F)
.

Observe that
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
is a certified-ED of
A^{\prime}
on
\widetilde{G}
, and it has the same quality parameters with those of
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
, except that routers in
{\cal R}^{\prime}
undergo one more batched update. Concretely, the quality of
L^{\prime}
is guaranteed by
Lemma

3.22
. The quality of
{\cal N^{\prime}}
follows
3.4
and the fact that
{\cal N}^{\prime}
is the restriction of
{\cal N}
on
A^{\prime}
.
\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}}
is a valid embedding on
\widetilde{G}
because we remove all router edges with embedding paths going through deleted edges by
Theorem

5.1
, and their embedding paths are removed in step 2.

Next, we can obtain a certified-ED
(C^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
of
A
on
\widetilde{G}
by applying
Lemma

6.10
on graph
\widetilde{G}
and
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
with the new node-weighting
A_{{\rm prune}}
. By
6.14
and
Lemma

6.10
, this certified-ED has the desired quality, except that it is for node-weighting
A
instead of
\widetilde{A}=A-\deg_{F}
, but this can be easily fixed. We update

{\cal N}^{\prime\prime}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
as follows. For each vertex
v\in V(F)
incident to some deleted edges, we choose arbitrary
\deg_{F}(v)
virtual nodes in
A(v)
, remove these virtual nodes from
\widetilde{\cal N}
, and mark the router vertices corresponding to them redundant. The final certified-ED is
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\cal N},\widetilde{\cal R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to\widetilde{G}})=(C^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{\cal N},{\cal R}^{\prime\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
.

6.14
.

|A_{{\rm prune}}|\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|
.

Proof.

First we have
|{\cal R}_{\mathrm{edge},F}|\leq|F|\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
because each deleted edge can destroy at most
\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
embedding paths. By definition,
\sum_{R\in{\cal R}}|R_{\mathrm{edge},F}|={\cal R}_{\mathrm{edge},F}
. By
Theorem

5.1
, each router
R\in{\cal R}
has
|R_{{\rm prune}}|\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot|R_{\mathrm{edge},F}|
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|A_{{\rm prune}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S\in{\cal S}}|S_{{\rm prune}}|\leq\sum_{R\in{\cal R}}|R_{{\rm prune}}|\leq\sum_{R\in{\cal R}}\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot|R_{\mathrm{edge},F}|\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|.

∎

The Recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
.

The recourse
\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})
can be bounded as follows. From
{\cal N}
to
{\cal N}^{\prime}
, the number of virtual node deletions is at most
|A_{{\rm prune}}|\cdot\omega
(each virtual node in
A_{{\rm prune}}
may be removed once for each clustering). The recourse from
{\cal N^{\prime}}
to
{\cal N}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
O((\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|A_{{\rm prune}}|)
by
Lemma

6.10
. Lastly, the recourse from
{\cal N}^{\prime\prime}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
is at most
O((\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|F|)
because each virtual node in
\deg_{F}
may appear in at most
\omega^{\prime\prime}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}
clusters. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})

\displaystyle=|A_{{\rm prune}}|\cdot\omega+O(|A_{{\rm prune}}|\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}))+O((\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|F|)

\displaystyle=O(\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|F|).

The Running Time.

Now we analyse the running time. The update from
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
to
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
is dominated by the edge deletion updates to routers in
{\cal R}
via
Theorem

5.1
. This step takes
2^{O(f)}\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|\cdot 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
time because the total size of edge deletions updates is
|{\cal R}_{\mathrm{edge},F}|\leq|F|\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}
(see the proof of
6.14
). From
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
to
(C^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
, it takes

|A_{{\rm prune}}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f+1)}\cdot\omega)

time by
Lemma

6.10
. Finally, updating
{\cal N}^{\prime\prime}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
takes
O((\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega})\cdot|F|)
. Therefore, the total running time is

\lambda_{\mathrm{rt},{\rm prune}}(f)\cdot\gamma_{{\rm emb}}\cdot|F|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega)
.

∎

6.4
Batched Edge Insertions

The update algorithm for a batched edge insertion is just a simple corollary of the node-weighting insertion algorithm, because we can just assign cut value
h
to all new edges to “block” them. A small point is that we need to add a new node-weighting
\deg_{E_{{\rm new}}}
to preserve the invariant
A\geq\deg_{G}+\mathds{1}(V(G))
.

6.15
.

Let
G
be a graph with a node-weighting
A\geq\deg_{G}+\mathds{1}(V(G))
and a certified-ED
(C,L,{\cal N},{\cal R},\Pi_{{\cal R}\to G})
of
A
on
G
in
Definition

6.1
.
Given a batch of edge insertions
E_{{\rm new}}
and parameters
\phi
and
h
, there is an algorithm that computes a certified-ED
(\widetilde{C},\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\cal N},\widetilde{\cal R},\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G})
of
\widetilde{A}=A+\deg_{E_{{\rm new}}}\geq\deg_{\widetilde{G}}
on
\widetilde{G}=G\cup E_{{\rm new}}
s.t.

•

\widetilde{C}=C+C_{{\rm new}}
with
|C_{{\rm new}}|\leq O((\kappa_{{\rm init},C}\cdot\phi+1)\cdot h\cdot|E_{{\rm new}}|)
.

•

\widetilde{L}=L\cup L_{{\rm new}}
with distortion
\widetilde{\sigma}=\sigma+O(h/\kappa_{\sigma})
and

|L_{{\rm new}}|=O(((\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},L}+\kappa_{{\rm init},L})\cdot\phi+1)\cdot|E_{{\rm new}}|).

•

\widetilde{\cal N}
is a
\widetilde{b}
-distributed
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}},\widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}},\widetilde{\omega})
-neighborhood cover of
\widetilde{A}
on
G-\widetilde{C}
with

\widetilde{b}=b+1,\ \widetilde{h}_{{\rm cov}}=h/3,\ \widetilde{h}_{\mathrm{sep}}=h/3,\text{ and }\widetilde{\omega}=\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}.

•

Routers in
\widetilde{\cal R}
are maintained by
Theorem

5.1
under
f+1
updates.

•

\Pi_{\widetilde{\cal R}\to G}
is a
(\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}},\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}})
-embedding with

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{{\rm emb}}=\max\{h_{{\rm emb}},O(\lambda_{{\rm init},h}\cdot h)\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{{\rm emb}}=\gamma_{{\rm emb}}+O((\omega\cdot\kappa_{\mathrm{CM},\gamma}+\kappa_{{\rm init},\gamma})/\phi).

The recourse from
{\cal N}
to
\widetilde{\cal N}
is
\mathrm{Rcs}({\cal N}\to\widetilde{\cal N})=O(|E_{{\rm new}}|\cdot(\omega+\kappa_{\mathrm{NC},\omega}))
.
The running time is

|E_{{\rm new}}|\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\phi+2^{O(f)}\cdot\omega)
.

Proof.

We first construct a certified-ED
(C^{\prime},L^{\prime},{\cal N}^{\prime},{\cal R}^{\prime},\Pi_{{\cal R}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{G}})
of
A
on
\widetilde{G}
by putting large cut values at the new edges. Precisely, we let

•

C^{\prime}=C+C_{E_{{\rm new}}}
, where
C_{E_{{\rm new}}}
assigns value
C_{E_{{\rm new}}}(e)=h
to each
e\in E_{{\rm new}}
,

•
