a completely new global vertex identifier. This step is to ensure that, over all time,
V(H_{x_{1},\mathrm{fresh}})
and
V(H_{x_{2},\mathrm{fresh}})
are disjoint for different
1\leq x_{1},x_{2}\leq\bar{x}
, and each
V(H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
is disjoint from
T
. For each
H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
, let
T_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}\subseteq V(H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
be vertices corresponding to
T\subseteq V(H_{x})
. Then we define
H
by setting
V(H)=T\cup\bigcup_{x}V(H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
and

E(H)=\{(v,v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})\mid v\in T,1\leq x\leq\bar{x}\}\cup\bigcup_{x}E(H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}),

where
v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}\in T_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
is the counterpart of
v\in T
, and each edge
(v,v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
has length
1
(if zero-length edges are allowed, it is more intuitive to set the length to be
0
). In other words, we take the union of all
H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
, add the terminals
T
, and then for each terminal
v\in T
and each
1\leq x\leq\bar{x}
, add an edge
(v,v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
connecting
v
to its counterpart
v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}\in T_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
.

To see the stretch of
H
, consider an arbitrary pair of different vertices
u,v\in T
. We first show that
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(u,v)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{up}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
for
\alpha_{\mathrm{up}}=\lambda^{x^{\star}-1}_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{up}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{up}}+2=2^{O(1/\epsilon^{2})}
. If
u
and
v
are not connected in
G
, then this statement trivially holds. Now suppose
u
and
v
are connected in
G
. We take the minimum
x^{\star}
s.t.
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)\leq h
. Note that
x^{\star}
must exist by
12.4
. Then for each
2\leq x\leq x^{\star}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x}}(u,v)\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{up}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x-1}}(u,v)/h
, which implies
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{up}}^{x^{\star}-1}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)/h^{x^{\star}-1}
. Because
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)\leq h
, the properties of
H^{\prime}_{x^{\star}}
gives
\mathrm{dist}_{H^{\prime}_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)\leq\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{up}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{H}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq 2+\mathrm{dist}_{H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}}(u_{x,\mathrm{fresh}},v_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})=2+\mathrm{dist}_{H_{x}}(u,v)

\displaystyle=2+\mathrm{dist}_{H^{\prime}_{x^{\star}}}(u,v)\cdot h^{x^{\star}-1}\leq 2+\lambda^{x^{\star}-1}_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{up}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{up}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq(\lambda^{x^{\star}-1}_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{up}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{up}}+2)\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v).

Next, we show that
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{H}(u,v)
, for
\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}=\lambda^{x^{\star}-1}_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\leq 2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
. We consider the shortest
u
-to-
v
path
P
on
H
. We decompose
P
into maximal subpaths
P^{\prime}
with both endpoints in
T
but no internal vertices in
T
. For each such subpath
P^{\prime}
(with endpoints
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in T
), we will show
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\ell_{H}(P^{\prime})
in a moment, so
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{H}(u,v)
holds by concatenating all such subpaths. Now consider one such subpath
P^{\prime}
. Note that there exists an
x
s.t.
P^{\prime}
is the concatenation of edge
(u^{\prime},u^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
, subpath
P^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
(connecting
u^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
and
v^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
) and edge
(v^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}},v^{\prime})
, where
P^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
is in
H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}
. This implies
\ell_{H}(P^{\prime})=2+\ell_{H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}}(P^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}^{x-1}\cdot h^{x-1}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G_{x}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}^{x-1}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot h^{x-1}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{H^{\prime}_{x}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}^{x-1}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{H_{x}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}^{x-1}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{H_{x,\mathrm{fresh}}}(u^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}},v^{\prime}_{x,\mathrm{fresh}})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{stk},\mathrm{low}}^{x^{\star}-1}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\ell_{H_{x}}(P^{\prime}),

where the first inequality is by property of distance-reduced graphs (see
Lemma

12.2
), and the second inequality is by the property of sparsifier
H^{\prime}_{x}
.

The running time is straightforward combining
Lemma

13.2
and the analysis in the proof of
Theorem

12.3
.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

13.1
.

The result
2
with worst-case update time in
Theorem

13.1
is a simple corollary from
Lemma

13.4
and
Lemma

12.6
by an argument similar to that in
Section

12.3
. We will maintain
2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
graphs
H_{1},...,H_{2^{O(1/\epsilon)}}
as candidates of the sparsifer instead of only one, because the reduction in
Lemma

12.6
basically obtains the fully dynamic algorithm by running
2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
instances of the online-batch dynamic algorithm in parallel, and at any moment, only one of the instances is ready for the up-to-date
G
. In other words, if we insist to maintain only one sparsifier
H
explicitly, then when we switch from the copy
k_{i-1}
for time
i-1
to the copy
k_{i}
for time
i
, the sparsifier
H
needs to change from
H_{k_{i-1}}
to
H_{k_{i}}
, and this may take
|H_{k_{i-1}}|+|H_{k_{i}}|=(|T^{(i-1)}|+|T^{(i)}|)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon^{4})}
(much larger than
n^{O(\epsilon)}
) in the worst case, which is unaffordable.

However, if amortized update time is allowed, then a much simpler transformation from online-batch dynamic algorithm to fully dynamic algorithm enables us to maintain the sparsifier
H
explicitly. Let
b=\lceil n^{\epsilon}\rceil
and
\bar{z}=\lceil 2/\epsilon\rceil
. For each integer
0\leq i\leq n^{2}
, we can represent it by a unique polynomial
i=\sum_{0\leq z\leq\bar{z}}a_{z}\cdot b^{\bar{z}-z}
, where
0\leq a_{z}\leq b-1
are integers. We initialize one instance of the online-batch dynamic algorithm, and keep the invariant that at any time
i
(if we arrive at a moment
i>n^{2}
, we just restart the algorithm), the algorithm is for batched updates
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)},...,\pi^{(\bar{z})}
where each
\pi^{(z)}
has size
|\pi^{(z)}|=a_{z}\cdot b^{\bar{z}-z}
(drop those
\pi^{(z)}
with size zero) and they are generated by chopping the current sequence of unit updates. By the invariant, when we move from moment
i-1
to
i
, we may drop a suffix of the batched updates and perform a new batch update (which is the union of the dropped batched updates and the unit update at moment
i
). To simulate this, we just need to withdraw the suffix of batched updates we are planning to drop (which takes time at most their total update time) and then perform the new batched update on the online-batch dynamic algorithm. Regarding the amortized update time, note that each unit update will be inside at most
\bar{z}\cdot b=n^{O(\epsilon)}
batched updates (including those got withdrew). Therefore, the total size of batched updates applied to the online-batch dynamic algorithm is at most the multiplication of
n^{O(\epsilon)}
and the length of the sequence of unit updates. Since the update time for a batch
\pi
is
|\pi|\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
, the final amortized update time is
n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}=n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

∎

14
Application: Maximum Multicommodity Flows

In this section, we show a constant-approximation multicommodity maxflows algorithm for vertex-unit-capacitated graphs. In the multicommodity maxflow problem, the input is
k
pairs of source and sink vertices, denoted by
\{(s_{j},t_{j})\mid 1\leq j\leq k\}
. Let
{\cal P}_{j}
collect all simple paths in
G
connecting
s_{j}
and
t_{j}
. The problem is formulated by the LP on the left below, and its dual is on the right.

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~\max

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\sum_{P\in{\cal P}_{j}}f(P)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\min\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{P\ni v}f(P)\leq 1,\forall v\in V(G)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t. }\sum_{v\in P}w(v)\geq 1,\forall P\in\bigcup_{j}{\cal P}_{j}

\displaystyle f(P)\geq 0,\forall P\in\bigcup_{j}{\cal P}_{j}~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~w(v)\geq 0,\forall v\in V(G)

14.1
.

Let
G
be an vertex-unit-capacitated undirected graph with
n
vertices,
m
edges, and
k
source sink pairs
\{(s_{j},t_{j})\mid 1\leq j\leq k\}
. For some sufficiently small constant
c>0
, given a parameter
1/\log^{c}n\leq\epsilon\leq 1
, there is a deterministic algorithm computes a
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
-approximate multicommodity maxflow in
O((m+n+k)n^{\epsilon})
time.

Proof.

We first prepare distance oracles (with simple-path-reporting interfaces) for graphs with vertex lengths. Precisely, let
G_{w}
be a graph with length function
w
on vertices. Naturally, for any simple path
P
on
G_{w}
, its length is
w(P)=\sum_{v\in P}w(v)
. For two vertices
u,v\in V(G_{w})
, the distance between them is the length of the shortest
u
-
v
simple paths.

To implement an oracle for vertex lengths, we define a graph
G_{\ell}
(isomorphic to
G_{w}
) with length function
\ell
on edges, where for each edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G_{\ell})
, define its length
\ell(e):=(w(u)+w(v))/2
. Obviously for each simple path
P
, we have
\ell(P)\leq w(P)\leq 2\ell(P)
, so any
\alpha^{\prime}
-approximate distance oracle for
G_{\ell}
is a
(2\alpha^{\prime})
-approximate distance oracle for
G_{w}
.

Therefore, applying the oracle in
Theorem

12.1
on
G_{\ell}
, we can obtain an
\alpha
-approximate dynamic distance oracle
{\cal O}
for
G_{w}
with approximate ratio
\alpha=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
. A small technical detail is that
Theorem

12.1
requires
G_{\ell}
has polynomially-bounded positive integral edge lengths. Fortunately, in our application in
Algorithm

2
, we can easily scale up the vertex lengths
w
to be real numbers (at least
1
and at most
\mathrm{poly}(n)
), round them up to integers, and multiply them by
2
. This will ensure edge lengths
\ell
are polynomially-bounded positive integral, and only increase the approximate ratio
\alpha
by a constant factor.

The Algorithm.

Given an
\alpha
-approximate deterministic dynamic distance oracle (with
simple
-path-reporting interfaces), it is quite standard to obtain fast algorithm for approximate multicommodity maxflows using a multiplicative weight update styled framework. The algorithm is shown in
Algorithm

2
. We use
G_{w}
to denote the graph
G
with function
w
as the edge length function. In
Algorithm

2
, we will initialized an oracle
{\cal O}
on
G_{w}
at line 2, query the oracle at line 7 and update the oracle at line 10.

Algorithm 2

Approximate Multicommodity Maxflows

1:

A vertex-unit-capacitated undirected graph
G
with
k
source-sink pairs
\{(s_{j},t_{j})\mid 1\leq j\leq k\}
and a constant
0<\delta<0.01
.

2:

A
((1+100\delta)\alpha^{2})
-approximate feasible multicommodity flow.

3:

Let
\zeta=1/\delta
and
\eta=\delta^{2}/((1+10\delta)\log n)
.

4:

Initialize
w(v)=1/n^{\zeta}
for all vertices
v\in V(G)
.

5:

Initialize
\lambda=1/n^{\zeta}
.

6:

Initialize
f
to be a zero flow.

7:

while

\lambda<1

do

8:

for
each source-sink pair
(s_{j},t_{j})

do

9:

Query an
\alpha
-approximate distance
\widetilde{d}
for
(s_{j},t_{j})
in
G_{w}
.

10:

If
\widetilde{d}>(1+\delta)\alpha\lambda
, then continue to the next source-sink pair.

11:

Query an
\alpha
-approximate shortest simple
s_{j}
-
t_{j}
path
P
in
G_{w}
.

12:

Add
P
into
f
with
f(P)=1
.

13:

For each vertex
v\in P
,
w(v)\leftarrow(1+\delta)\cdot w(v)
.

14:

end

for

15:

\lambda\leftarrow(1+\delta)\lambda

16:

end

while

17:

Return
f\cdot\eta
.

14.2
.

At the moment an
\alpha
-approximate shortest simple
s_{j}
-
t_{j}
path
P
is obtained, we have
w(P)\leq(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}\lambda
.

Proof.

This is from

w(P)\leq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{dist}_{G_{w}}(s_{j},t_{j})\leq\alpha\cdot\widetilde{d}\leq(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}\lambda,

where
\widetilde{d}\leq(1+\delta)\alpha\lambda
is by line 8.
∎

The Correctness.

The proof of correctness below follows the presentation of
[
HHT23
]
. Combining
Lemmas

14.4
and
14.5
and
\alpha=2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
, we have that
f\cdot\eta
is a
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
-approximate feasible solution.

14.3
.

At any time, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{w}}(s_{j},t_{j})\geq\lambda
for each
1\leq j\leq k
.

Proof.

This statement holds initially. Every time
\lambda
is updated to
(1+\delta)\lambda
, the exit condition of the inner loop guarantees that
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{w}}(s_{j},t_{j})>(1+\delta)\lambda
for each pair
(s_{j},t_{j})
, because the
\alpha
-approximate distance
\widetilde{d}
of
(s_{j},t_{j})
has
w(P)>(1+\delta)\alpha\lambda
. Therefore, this statement still holds after each update of
\lambda
.
∎

14.4
.

The flow
f\cdot\eta
is feasible.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
f(v)\leq 1/\eta
for each vertex
v\in V(G)
. By the update rule, the final weight of
v
is
w_{\mathrm{final}}(v)=(1+\delta)^{f(v)}/n^{\zeta}
. Moreover,
w_{\mathrm{final}}(v)\leq(1+\delta)^{2}\alpha
, because right before the last time
w(v)
is updated,
w(v)\leq w(P)\leq(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}\lambda
(by
14.2
) and
\lambda\leq 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(1+\delta)^{f(v)}/n^{\zeta}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)^{2}\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle(1+\delta)^{f(v)}

\displaystyle\leq n^{\zeta+1}

\displaystyle f(v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\zeta+1)\log n}{\log(1+\delta)}

\displaystyle f(v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\delta)(\zeta+1)\log n}{\delta}

\displaystyle f(v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\delta)^{2}\log n}{\delta^{2}},

where we assume
(1+\delta)^{2}\alpha^{2}\leq n
in the second line, and uses
\delta/(1+\delta)\leq\log(1+\delta)
for all
\delta>-1
in the fourth line. Therefore,
f(v)\leq 1/\eta
by our choice of
\eta
.
∎

14.5
.

The flow
f\cdot\eta
is an
((1+100\delta)\cdot\alpha^{2})
-approximate multicommodity maxflow.

Proof.

Let
\beta
denote the value of the maxflow.
Consider an iteration
i
(we refer to the inner loop as one iteration). Let
\lambda^{(i)},w^{(i)}
denote the variable
\lambda
and function
w
at the beginning of this iteration. Let
D^{(i)}=\sum w^{(i)}(v)
. Note that
w^{(i)}/\lambda^{(i)}
is a feasible dual solution, so
\beta\leq D^{(i)}/\lambda^{(i)}
. Let
P_{i}
be the path
P
in iteration
i
. By our update rule, we have

\displaystyle D^{(i+1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\notin P_{i}}w^{(i)}(v)+\sum_{v\in P_{i}}(1+\delta)\cdot w^{(i)}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v}w^{(i)}(v)+\sum_{v\in P_{i}}\delta\cdot w^{(i)}(v)

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}+\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}\lambda^{(i)}

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}\cdot(1+\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}/\beta)

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}\cdot\exp(\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}/\beta).

From the second line to the third line, we use
w^{(i)}(P_{i})\leq(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}\lambda^{(i)}
from
14.2
.

Let
K=\mathrm{val}(f)
and let
T-1
be the last iteration, then the final dual value satisfies

D^{(T)}\leq D^{(1)}\cdot\exp(\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}K/\beta).

From the initialization, we have
D^{(1)}=1/n^{\zeta-1}
. At the end of the algorithm, we have
\min\mathrm{dist}_{G_{w}}(s_{j},t_{j})\geq\lambda\geq 1
(by
Lemma

14.3
), which implies
D^{(T)}\geq 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle n^{\zeta-1}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}K/\beta)

\displaystyle\beta\log n\cdot\frac{\zeta-1}{\delta(1+\delta)\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq K

\displaystyle\frac{\beta(1-\delta)}{(1+\delta)(1+10\delta)\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq K\eta

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(1+100\delta)\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq K\eta

which means
\mathrm{val}(f\cdot\eta)=K\eta\geq\beta/((1+100\delta)\alpha^{2})
.
∎

The Running Time.

The running time is dominated by the total update time, total distance query time and total path-reporting time of the oracle
{\cal O}
on
G_{\ell}
.

Let
{\cal P}_{\mathrm{report}}
collect all paths obtained at line 9. We have the total number of edges on paths in
{\cal P}_{\mathrm{report}}
is at most
O(n\log n)
, i.e.
\sum_{P\in{\cal P}_{\mathrm{report}}}|P|\leq O(n\log n)
by the fact that
f\cdot\eta
is a feasible flow and
\eta=\Theta(1/\log n)
. Therefore, the total path-reporting time is
O(n^{1+\epsilon})
by
Theorem

12.1
.

For the total distance query time, note that each distance query followed by a path-reporting query will have its distance query time
O(\log\log n/\epsilon^{4})
dominated by the path-reporting time
O(|P|\cdot n^{\epsilon})
because
|P|\geq 1
. Hence, we only need to consider the extra distance queries of those inner-loop iterations ended at line 8. There are at most
O(k\cdot\log_{1+\delta}n^{\zeta})=O(k\log n)
such iterations, so the extra distance queries takes
O(k\log n\log\log n/\epsilon^{4})
time.

Finally, we bound the total update time. For each vertex
v\in V(G)
, its weight
w(v)
will be updated at most
O(\log_{1+\delta}n^{\delta})=O(\log n)
time. Each update to
w(v)
will change the edge lengths of
\deg_{G}(v)
edges in
G_{\ell}
. To change the length of some edge in
G_{\ell}
, we can remove the edge and add it back with new edge length. Therefore, the total number of updates to
G_{\ell}
is
O(m\log n)
, and the total update time is
O(mn^{\epsilon})
.

In conclusion, the total running time is
O(n^{1+\epsilon}+k\log n\log\log n/\epsilon^{4}+mn^{\epsilon})=O((m+n+k)n^{\epsilon})
.

∎
